- **Why it matters:** Offline device, user runs `timeset 2026-07-28 09:00:00`. `cmd_timeset` (System_Utils.cpp:1969-1971) calls `settimeofday` and, on RTC boards, `rtcSyncFromSystem()` — neither calls `timeSyncUpdateBootEpoch()`. The wall clock is now correct, but `gBootEpochUsOffset` still encodes "epoch == uptime", so `getTimestampPrefixMsCached` computes `epochUs ~= uptime_us`, `Clock::isValidEpoch(sec)` fails, and it returns an empty prefix. Every line in successful_login.log / failed_login.log / errors.log / i2c_errors.log keeps stamping `[ms=N]` instead of the wall clock, indefinitely. Same on an RTC-equipped board with no WiFi: `rtcEarlyBootSync()` gives a valid clock at :1536 but the offset was already latched at :1448 and never re-derived.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED — prior art: Adjacent to recon_prior 2.1 ("the viewer cannot parse the four always-on admin logs; the `[ms=N]` no-NTP fallback fails too") — that item is about the log FORMAT. This is a distinct root cause for why
- **Direction:** Call `timeSyncUpdateBootEpoch()` from every `settimeofday()` site (cmd_timeset, rtcEarlyBootSync, rtcSyncToSystem), and change the guard inside it to `Clock::isSynced()` so an unsynced clock leaves the offset at 0 and the lazy refresh keeps retrying.

### [MEDIUM] memreport can never match sensor_queue_task: the name is 17 chars and FreeRTOS truncates task names to 15
- **Status: RESOLVED 2026-08-23.** `appTasks[]` and both `strcmp` sites are deleted; `memreport` classifies by `taskStackLookup()`, which compares under the same `configMAX_TASK_NAME_LEN - 1` bound FreeRTOS truncates to, and the size printed is the measured allocator block, not the table constant.
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/System_Utils.cpp:3693`
- **Also:** strcmp sites System_Utils.cpp:3712 and :3751; create sites HardwareOne.cpp:1611 and System_I2C.cpp:3052 both pass the literal "sensor_queue_task"
- **What:** appTasks[] holds the literal "sensor_queue_task" (17 chars). sdkconfig:1982 sets CONFIG_FREERTOS_MAX_TASK_NAME_LEN=16, so pcTaskName stores 15 chars + NUL = "sensor_queue_ta". The strcmp at :3712 therefore never returns 0. It is the only one of the 38 firmware task names longer than 15 chars (g2_wifi_pending and g2_ring_pending are exactly 15 and fit).
- **Why it matters:** The task is silently classified as a system task: it is printed with HWM only (no allocated size, no % used) and its 4096 B is excluded from 'Subtotal (app)' and from that subtotal's roll-up into static_total. System_TaskUtils.h's own comment records that sensor_queue_task was once observed CRITICAL at ~79% peak — that is precisely the task the report drops. The second strcmp at :3751 has the same effect in reverse, so it also shows up in the system-task list where the '4 KB assumed' fiction applies.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED
- **Direction:** Either shorten the created name to <= 15 chars at both create sites, or match with strncmp against CONFIG_FREERTOS_MAX_TASK_NAME_LEN-1. Better: key the table on TaskHandle_t like the other two reporters already do.

### [MEDIUM] cmd_factoryreset deletes users.json and then schedules its reboot through an unchecked esp_timer_create/start_once pair
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/System_Utils.cpp:2218`
- **What:** After VFS::removeGuarded(USERS_JSON_FILE, ...) succeeds, the command calls `esp_timer_create(&timerArgs, &timer); esp_timer_start_once(timer, 1000ULL * 1000ULL);` (System_Utils.cpp:2218-2219) with both esp_err_t values discarded. `timer` is initialized to nullptr, so a failed create leaves it null.
- **Why it matters:** esp_timer_create allocates, so it fails with ESP_ERR_NO_MEM under heap pressure — and factoryreset is a command a user reaches for precisely when the device is misbehaving. esp_timer_start_once(NULL, ...) does not crash (esp_timer.c:182-184 returns ESP_ERR_INVALID_ARG), so the failure is completely silent: the command returns "Factory reset complete. ... Rebooting in 1 second to start setup wizard...", posts SYSEVT_FACTORY_RESET and writes the REBOOT audit line, and the device keeps running with its user database already deleted and no reboot pending. Every subsequent auth attempt fails and the setup wizard cannot run because detectFirstTimeSetupState only re-evaluates at boot. The user, told a reboot is imminent, has no reason to suspect anything.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED
- **Direction:** Check both returns and fall back to an immediate esp_restart() (or return an explicit error telling the user to power-cycle) rather than reporting a reboot that was never armed.

### [MEDIUM] Monotonic-to-wall-clock offset for log timestamps is latched once and never refreshed after settimeofday(), so RTC-only boots log boot-relative time forever
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/System_Utils.cpp:760`
- **Also:** components/hardwareone/i2csensor_ds3231.cpp:210, :245; components/hardwareone/System_Utils.cpp:1987
- **What:** getTimestampPrefixMsCached() lazily computes gBootEpochUsOffset = time(nullptr)*1e6 - esp_timer_get_time() the first time it is called and only when the offset is still 0. timeSyncUpdateBootEpoch() has exactly one real call site outside that lazy path — System_Debug.cpp:2319 inside logTimeSyncedMarkerIfReady(), which is itself called only from the NTP-success branch (System_Utils.cpp:2418) and self-latches on gTimeSyncedMarkerWritten. None of the three settimeofday() call sites refresh it: i2csensor_ds3231.cpp:210 (rtcEarlyBootSync), :245 (rtcSyncToSystem), System_Utils.cpp:1987 (cmd_timeset).
- **Why it matters:** On ESP-IDF the system clock starts at epoch 0 and advances with uptime, so time(nullptr) is already non-zero (== uptime seconds) long before any real sync. Boot order in HardwareOne.cpp: Serial.begin + delay(500) at :1327, initFilesystem at :1335, then logBootAnchorToLogs() at :1485 -> buildTimestampPrefix() -> getTimestampPrefixMsCached(). By :1485 uptime is well past 1 s, so time(nullptr) >= 1 and the offset latches to a bogus near-zero value. rtcEarlyBootSync() only runs afterwards, at :1576. From then on epochUs = staleOffset + esp_timer ~= uptime, Clock::isValidEpoch() rejects it (year < 2020), and getTimestampPrefixMsCached() returns "" for the rest of the boot. Every file-log sink then falls back to the `[ms=N]` / `[BOOT ms=N]` prefix: errors.log, system-events.log, i2c_errors.log, successful/failed_login.log, sensorlog rows (System_SensorLogging.cpp:337), task logs (System_TaskUtils.cpp:116/387) and the web audit log (WebServer_Server.cpp:403/1497). On a WiFi device the NTP branch repairs it; on an offline device whose clock comes from the DS3231 it never does. Second, milder form on every board: after the one-shot marker is written, a later `timeset` or `rtcsync tosystem` steps the wall clock while gBootEpochUsOffset stays pinned, so log timestamps and Clock::* timestamps disagree by exactly the step.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED — board: all (the permanent form needs a non-NTP clock source: ENABLE_RTC_SENSOR builds, or any board where the user runs `timeset`); prior art: Adjacent to the log-viewer regex issue in docs/DOCS_ACCURACY_AUDIT_2026-07-26.md §2.1 (which notes the `[ms=N]` fallback is unparseable) — the cause of the fallback firing is not documented anywhere.
- **Direction:** Call timeSyncUpdateBootEpoch() from every settimeofday() site, and gate the lazy latch on Clock::isSynced() rather than `gBootEpochUsOffset == 0` so a pre-sync call cannot poison the cache.

### [MEDIUM] The hot logging path runs a whole-filesystem littlefs traversal (LittleFS.usedBytes) up to every 2 seconds, and the code comments claim it is cheap
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/System_VFS.cpp:560`
- **Also:** Callers: System_Filesystem.cpp:1820 (appendLineWithCap, i.e. every ERROR/EVENT/EVLOG line and every audited command), System_SensorLogging.cpp:214/247/891, System_Battery.cpp (batteryLogAppend via resolveOverflowPath), System_Automation.cpp:2826 (FSFREE condition variable). Direct unmediated calls: System_Utils.cpp:2007, System_Filesystem.cpp:199/1214/1222, System_FileManager.cpp:396, System_ImageManager.cpp:200/501, System_SensorLogging.cpp:1178, System_VFS.cpp:515.
- **What:** refreshLittleFsFreeCached() caches for 2 s (or 32 KB of writes) and then calls LittleFS.totalBytes()/LittleFS.usedBytes() under FsLockGuard. usedBytes maps to esp_littlefs_info, which computes block_size * lfs_fs_size(fs) (esp_littlefs.c:295). lfs_fs_size_ -> lfs_fs_traverse_ (lfs.c:5179-5187, 4693-4790) walks every metadata pair with lfs_dir_fetch AND calls lfs_ctz_traverse for every regular file, reading the CTZ skip-list pointer at the head of every allocated block of every file. resolveOverflowPath() calls this on the way into every capped-log append.
- **Why it matters:** Each of those pointer reads is a 4-8 byte lfs_bd_read at offset 0 of a different block, so it always misses the 512 B rcache and issues a fresh esp_partition_read -> esp_flash_read -> spi1_start -> cache_disable. Every one of those is a full dual-core stop: IPC-wake the other core's ipc task, suspend both schedulers, mask non-IRAM interrupts, disable cache, read ~128 B, restore. With the capped logs alone at their limits (256+256+256+680+680+64+500 KB = 2.7 MB, ~670 blocks) one usedBytes() call means several hundred of these micro-stalls; on a well-filled 10.6 MB volume it is ~2600. That is roughly 6-50 ms of chopped-up dual-core unavailability, holding gFsMutex, recurring at up to 0.5 Hz for as long as anything is logging — and it grows with disk fill, i.e. it gets worse exactly when the free-space check matters. The tree contradicts itself on this: System_Filesystem.cpp:1037 explicitly refuses to call it per chunk because "LittleFS.usedBytes() scans the FS and would add that cost to every one of the thousands of chunks", while System_Automation.cpp:2825 labels the same call "cached read, scheduler-safe". Only the first comment is correct. Note the value is used solely to answer "is free space below 100 KB?" (LOG_OVERFLOW_DEFAULT_RESERVE, System_VFS.cpp:533).
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED
- **Direction:** Cheapen or slow down the refresh: lengthen the 2 s window substantially (the latch is one-way per boot and the threshold is a coarse 100 KB), seed a running free-space estimate from noteLittleFsBytesWritten/remove and only re-scan on a large drift, or hoist the scan onto a low-priority housekeeping task instead of running it inline under gFsMutex in the logging path.

### [MEDIUM] `sddiag` (non-admin) re-muxes the camera XCLK pin GPIO10 to SPI MOSI and tears down the SD SPI bus without restoring it
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/System_VFS.cpp:1155`
- **Also:** components/hardwareone/System_VFS.cpp:1174 (Alt-2 testSDPins(21,7,8,10)), :1063-1120 (testSDPins body), :1209 (registered with requiresAdmin=false); components/hardwareone/System_Camera_DVP.cpp:151 (XCLK_GPIO_NUM 10), :241
- **What:** cmd_sddiag reads raw pin states with `int pins[] = {3, 7, 8, 9, 10, 21}; for (int p : pins) { pinMode(p, INPUT); ... }`, then, if the configured pin set fails to answer, falls back to `testSDPins(21, 7, 8, 10)` which calls SPI.begin(sck=7, miso=8, mosi=10, cs=21). GPIO10 is XCLK_GPIO_NUM, the camera master clock, driven as an LEDC output (managed_components/espressif__esp32-camera/target/xclk.c:50-61 does ledc_channel_config on config.pin_xclk). testSDPins also opens with SPI.end() and never re-runs the SD mount sequence.
- **Why it matters:** Running `sddiag` on a XIAO Sense while the camera is initialized detaches the LEDC channel from GPIO10 (pinMode) and then, on the fallback path, routes SPI MOSI onto the same pad. The OV2640/OV3660 loses its master clock, frames stop arriving, and the driver goes into the FB-OVF/blocking-esp_camera_fb_get state the file's own boot-safety comment at System_Camera_DVP.cpp:186-199 describes. Nothing restores the LEDC channel or the SD SPI pin set, so a mounted /sd is also left on a torn-down bus. The command is registered with requiresAdmin=false, so any authenticated user can trigger it.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED — board: Seeed XIAO ESP32-S3 Sense only (the only board where SD_CS_PIN is defined)
- **Direction:** Gate the alternative-pin probes behind an explicit opt-in argument, skip any candidate pin that is already claimed by an enabled subsystem (camera XCLK/SCCB/data), and restore the configured SPI pin set plus re-mount /sd before returning. Making the command admin-only would also be reasonable given it drives hardware.

### [MEDIUM] cmd_certgen holds the generated HTTPS private key PEM in PSRAM and frees it without zeroization
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/System_WiFi.cpp:1512`
- **Also:** docs/LAZY_ALLOCATION_AUDIT.md notes the sibling issue that the HTTPS PEM Strings (~2.5 KB) survive closehttp — a lifetime finding, not this placement/zeroization one.
- **What:** cmd_certgen allocates keyPem = heap_caps_malloc(4096, MALLOC_CAP_SPIRAM) (:1512) and fills it with mbedtls_pk_write_key_pem (:1524) — the plaintext PKCS#8/SEC1 private key. The mbedtls_entropy_context and mbedtls_ctr_drbg_context are likewise PSRAM (:1405-1406). The cleanup block (:1562-1572) calls heap_caps_free(keyPem) with no mbedtls_platform_zeroize first, so the key text stays in PSRAM until that block is reallocated and overwritten.
- **Why it matters:** Flash encryption is off on this project, so PSRAM is plaintext on an externally probeable chip, and heap_caps_free does not clear the block. After running 'certgen' the complete server private key sits recoverable in PSRAM until something else happens to allocate and overwrite those 4 KB. This directly contradicts the project's standing 'never put secrets in PSRAM' rule. Note the exposure is bounded: the long-lived copy is safe - sHttpsKeyData (WebServer_Server.cpp:5131/5188) is an Arduino String whose backing buffer is a few hundred bytes to ~1.7 KB, well under CONFIG_SPIRAM_MALLOC_ALWAYSINTERNAL=16384, so it stays in internal DRAM; and the mbedtls key material itself (mpi limbs) is calloc'd small and therefore also internal. Only the PEM staging buffer leaks to PSRAM.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED — independently reported by 3 finders; board: all boards with ENABLE_HTTPS; prior art: Adjacent and the prior doc is wrong on status: docs/LAZY_ALLOCATION_AUDIT.md:33 lists 'the HTTPS private key' among things that must not be PSRAM-moved, implying it is not there. It already is, on thi
- **Direction:** Allocate keyPem with MALLOC_CAP_INTERNAL|MALLOC_CAP_8BIT (4 KB, transient, and the command is admin-only so contention is not a concern), and zeroize it with mbedtls_platform_zeroize before free at the cleanup label. Leaving certPem in PSRAM is fine - it is public.

### [MEDIUM] setupWiFi() can stall the entire boot for minutes with no overall budget, and unlike the NTP wait it never refreshes the OLED
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/System_WiFi.cpp:300`
- **Also:** System_WiFi.cpp:964 (kMaxWifiAttempts), :1064 (IDLE-bug timer reset), :1114 (narrow early bail)
- **What:** `connectToBestWiFiNetwork()` (System_WiFi.cpp:300-309) walks every saved network — `MAX_WIFI_NETWORKS` is 8 (System_WiFi.h:9) — with a 2000 ms pause between them, calling `connectWiFiIndex(i, 12000, true)`. Inside, `kMaxWifiAttempts = 3` (:964) with 1500 ms then 3000 ms backoff, 500 ms post-connect settle, and a 12 s status-poll loop (:996). The IDLE-bug handler at :1064 resets `start = millis()` mid-loop, granting one extra full 12 s window per attempt. The early bail at :1114 only covers WL_STOPPED / WL_NO_SHIELD; an out-of-range AP (WL_NO_SSID_AVAIL / WL_DISCONNECTED) runs the full timeout every time. Nothing above this caps total boot WiFi time.
- **Why it matters:** Eight saved networks, none in range, is roughly 8 x (2 s + 3 x 12.5 s + 4.5 s) ≈ 5.5 minutes, and up to ~10 minutes if the IDLE-bug path triggers per attempt. `hardwareone_setup()` blocks at :1703 for that whole window, so nothing past it starts: no device discovery, no Bluetooth, no sensors, no HTTP, no ESP-NOW, no MQTT — and `hardwareone_loop()` has not begun, so there is no OLED refresh, no powerSaveTick, no automation tick. The status loop at :996-1083 contains no `oledUpdate()`, so the panel freezes on "Connecting WiFi" at 30% and looks hung. The contrast is right next door: `syncNTPAndResolve()` (System_Utils.cpp:2376-2383) caps itself at 15 s *and* calls `oledUpdate()` on every 100 ms iteration specifically to keep the boot animation alive. The only escape is `Serial.available()` at :1076, which requires a USB-attached operator — precisely the person who is not there in the documented "FeatherS3 out-and-about" scenario.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED — board: all with ENABLE_WIFI
- **Direction:** Add a single wall-clock budget around the whole boot WiFi phase (the per-network and per-attempt limits do not compose into one), and call oledUpdate() inside the status-poll loop the way the NTP wait already does so the panel does not read as a hang.

### [MEDIUM] Boot blocks in setupWiFi() for up to ~6 minutes with the OLED progress screen frozen; the comment claiming the animation keeps running is false
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/System_WiFi.cpp:300`
- **Also:** System_WiFi.cpp:966-1084 (attempt loop + 12 s poll + IDLE-bug reset at :1029/:1064); HardwareOne.cpp:1643 (the inaccurate comment), :1739-1746
- **What:** `connectToBestWiFiNetwork()` walks up to MAX_WIFI_NETWORKS (8, System_WiFi.h:9) saved networks with a `delay(2000)` between them. Each network runs `connectWiFiIndex(i, 12000, true)`, which does `wifiEnsureIdle(200)` + a ~600 ms driver stop/start, then three attempts of `delay(500)` plus a 12 000 ms status poll, with 1500 ms and 3000 ms backoffs between attempts. The `hitIdleBug` branch resets `start = millis()` (:1029, :1064), granting each attempt a second full 12 s window. Worst case per network is ~42 s normally and ~78 s if the IDLE branch fires on every attempt; across 8 networks that is ~355 s to ~640 s. Throughout, `hardwareone_loop()` has not started and the connect poll loop never calls `oledUpdate()` — unlike the NTP wait immediately after it, which does exactly that with the comment "Keep boot animation running during NTP wait" (System_Utils.cpp:2396-2398).
- **Why it matters:** A headless unit powered up out of range of every saved network sits for minutes showing a static "Connecting WiFi... 30%" frame (rendered once by `oledSetBootProgress` at OLED_Utils.cpp:6550-6558 — there is no OLED task, so nothing repaints it) with no HTTP, no ESP-NOW, no CLI and no watchdog that could shorten it. HardwareOne.cpp:1643 asserts "OLED early init — boot animation runs during slow WiFi/NTP phases below", which is true for NTP and false for WiFi; `oledApplySettings()` logs "boot animation running" for the same reason. A user watching a frozen 30% bar for six minutes has no way to distinguish it from the wedge in the finding above.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED — board: all (ENABLE_WIFI)
- **Direction:** Call `oledUpdate()` inside the `connectWiFiIndex` status-poll loop the way the NTP wait already does, and consider a total boot-WiFi budget (e.g. stop after the first N networks or a wall-clock cap) rather than a per-network timeout multiplied by eight. The serial escape at System_WiFi.cpp:1076 is real and works; nothing equivalent exists for a headless or display-only unit.

### [LOW] `crashCopyStr`'s `IRAM_ATTR` rests on a false premise and buys nothing but IRAM on a build documented as IRAM-full
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/System_CrashRecord.cpp:93`
- **Also:** components/hardwareone/CMakeLists.txt:467-470 -- the comment claims "System_CrashRecord.cpp defines __wrap_esp_panic_handler()". It does not; it rides Arduino's via `set_arduino_panic_handler` (:176), exactly as System_CrashRecord.h:63-67 correctly explains. The `-Wl,--wrap` flag at :475 is still required, so only the comment is wrong.
- **What:** This is the only `IRAM_ATTR` and the only IRAM/DRAM placement attribute in all of `components/hardwareone/` + `main/`. The comment at :90-92 justifies it as "Used from panic context, where the discipline is to call as little as possible -- this avoids depending on where libc's strncpy happens to live". But every frame above it is flash-resident: `panic_handler()`/`esp_panic_handler()` (esp_system, and `# CONFIG_ESP_PANIC_HANDLER_IRAM is not set` at sdkconfig:1799), Arduino's `__wrap_esp_panic_handler` and `handle_custom_backtrace` (components/arduino/cores/esp32/esp32-hal-misc.c:418 / :361), and `crashPanicHook` itself (:125) are all in flash. Control could not have arrived at `crashCopyStr` with the cache off. And it never is off: `panic_enable_cache()` (esp-idf/components/esp_system/port/panic_handler.c:274) is called from `panicHandler()` :294 and `xt_unhandled_exception()` :307 *before* `panic_handler()` runs.
- **Why it matters:** No correctness bug -- the function is fine. The cost is IRAM on a build whose own docs say "IRAM goes to ~100 % full with BT on" (docs/MEMORY_LAYOUT.md:146), spent for zero safety. The larger cost is that the rationale is wrong in a file whose entire value proposition is being trustworthy about panic context: the next person adding a panic-time helper will copy the attribute and the reasoning, and may conclude that flash rodata is unsafe there (it isn't) or that IRAM placement of a leaf makes a flash-resident caller safe (it doesn't).
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED
- **Direction:** Drop `IRAM_ATTR` and keep the hand-rolled loop (avoiding libc in panic context is still worth it for a different reason -- call-depth). Replace the rationale with the real one: `panic_enable_cache()` guarantees the cache is on, so rodata and flash-resident code are both fine here. Fix the CMakeLists comment while touching it.

### [LOW] The only IRAM_ATTR function in the tree is called exclusively from a flash-resident caller, so the placement costs IRAM for no benefit
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/System_CrashRecord.cpp:93`
- **What:** `crashCopyStr` is declared `static void IRAM_ATTR` (:93). Its only two call sites are inside `crashPanicHook` (:159, :164), which is a plain flash-resident function — as the file's own comment acknowledges (:120-124: "the flash cache has been re-enabled by panic_enable_cache(). So reading rodata and doing plain stores is fine"). It also reads `g_panic_abort_details` and the result of `esp_app_get_elf_sha256_str()`, both flash/rodata-backed, from the non-IRAM caller.
- **Why it matters:** If the cache were ever actually off in this context, the caller would fault before reaching the IRAM leaf, so the attribute buys nothing; if it is on (which the comment establishes it is), the attribute is unnecessary. Net effect is IRAM consumed out of the DRAM pool on a build the tree documents as running IRAM near full (docs/MEMORY_LAYOUT.md:146). This also invalidates the recon map's claim of zero IRAM_ATTR in first-party code — System_CrashRecord.cpp is new and untracked.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED
- **Direction:** Drop the IRAM_ATTR, or keep it and say in the comment that it is defensive-only. Either way the comment at :120-124 should be the single source of truth about what the panic context permits.

### [LOW] Crash-record repeat counter says "in a row" but healthy boots do not reset it
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/System_CrashRecord.cpp:277`
- **Also:** components/hardwareone/System_CrashRecord.cpp:361-362,394-400 (the rendered wording)
- **What:** crashRecordBootConsume only enters the repeat-tracking block when sSignature is non-zero, which requires isFaultReset(resetReason) (:264-275). A clean reboot or power-on yields sSignature == 0, so rtcLastSignature and rtcRepeatCount are left untouched. crashRecordMarkBootHealthy (:394) clears rtcConsecutive but deliberately does not clear them either.
- **Why it matters:** Crash with signature S, run healthy for a week, reboot cleanly several times, then crash once more with the same S: rtcRepeatCount increments to 2 and both crashRecordEmitEarly ("sig=0x… x2") and crashRecordDetail ("signature : 0x… seen x2 in a row") report a recurring crash loop. For a diagnostic whose whole job is to distinguish a one-off from a loop, that inverts the answer — and it is the boot banner an operator reads first.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED
- **Direction:** Clear rtcLastSignature/rtcRepeatCount in crashRecordMarkBootHealthy alongside rtcConsecutive, or relabel the field to "seen N times since power-on" so the text matches what is actually counted.

### [LOW] debug_out task creation carries a stale "~14KB stack" comment; the constant is 4 KB and is already 81% used
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/System_Debug.cpp:663`
- **What:** `initDebugSystem()` passes `DEBUG_OUT_STACK_WORDS` to xTaskCreatePinnedToCore annotated `// ~14KB stack — see System_TaskUtils.h for sizing rationale`. The referenced constant is `constexpr uint32_t DEBUG_OUT_STACK_WORDS = 4096;` (System_TaskUtils.h:67), i.e. 4 KB of bytes, and the header's own note on that line reads "(was annotated 16KB — 4x wrong)" followed by "HW-OBSERVED 81% USED (~764 B free)".
- **Why it matters:** Anyone sizing work into debugOutputTask from the creation site believes there is roughly 3.5x more headroom than exists — on the one task that does LittleFS appends, log rotation and vsnprintf formatting (System_Debug.cpp:250-280), and that the header already flags as the tightest task in the build. This is the same class as the three sites already catalogued in the task recon (G2_Glasses.cpp:11424, :8917 and System_Camera_DVP.cpp:988) but is a fourth, previously unlisted site.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED — prior art: KNOWN class — see the *_STACK_WORDS byte/word note in components/hardwareone/System_TaskUtils.h:9-29; this specific site is new
- **Direction:** Correct the comment to 4 KB and carry the header's 81%-used warning to the creation site, since that is where a future caller decides what is safe to do on this task.

### [LOW] Every `*FromISR` call in the tree passes NULL for `pxHigherPriorityTaskWoken` and there is no `portYIELD_FROM_ISR` anywhere
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/System_Debug.cpp:752`
- **What:** The only `*FromISR` uses in `components/hardwareone/` + `main/` are the four in `enqueueChunk`: `xQueueReceiveFromISR(gDebugFreeQueue, &msg, NULL)` (:752), `xQueueSendFromISR(gDebugOutputQueue, &msg, NULL)` (:764) and `xQueueSendFromISR(gDebugFreeQueue, &msg, NULL)` (:767). All pass NULL for the higher-priority-task-woken out-parameter, and a repo-wide grep for `portYIELD_FROM_ISR` across first-party code returns zero hits.
- **Why it matters:** If the ISR branch ever executes, the send that unblocks the `debug_out` consumer never requests a context switch, so the wake is deferred to the next scheduler tick instead of happening on ISR exit. On a burst that would let the free-slot pool drain and turn into `incrementDebugDropped()` losses that look like queue saturation rather than a missing yield. Same reachability caveat as the PSRAM finding: no first-party ISR calls this today, so nothing is losing messages right now.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED — prior art: recon_isr.md section 6 raised this as a "P15 nit"; I confirmed it independently and it is not in any docs/*.md
- **Direction:** If the ISR branch survives the PSRAM decision above, thread a `BaseType_t xHigherPriorityTaskWoken = pdFALSE` through all four calls and `portYIELD_FROM_ISR(xHigherPriorityTaskWoken)` before returning. If the branch is deleted, this goes away with it -- prefer that.

### [LOW] ESP-SR voice-arm state is mutated without the lock whenever the mutex take times out
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/System_ESPSR.cpp:2805`
- **Also:** ensureMNCommandMutex (:1508-1511) is the same lazy check-then-act shape.
- **What:** Every arm/disarm site is written as `if (gVoiceArmMutex && xSemaphoreTake(..., 200ms) == pdTRUE) { mutate; give; } else { mutate; }` — see :2802-2807 (sr start), :2837-2842 (sr stop) and the same pattern at :2850+. On timeout the mutation runs unsynchronized, including `gVoiceArmedUser = ctx.user` (an Arduino String assignment, so a potential realloc/free). The broadcastOutput at :2811 and the String append at :2819 read gVoiceArmedUser outside the lock unconditionally. `ensureVoiceArmMutex()` (:120-124) is also an unguarded check-then-act singleton.
- **Why it matters:** The reader `isVoiceArmed()` (:165-174) runs on the SR task and correctly takes the mutex to copy the username out — but a lock a writer is permitted to skip protects nothing. A 200 ms contention means core-0 cmd_exec rewrites gVoiceArmedUser's heap buffer while the SR task copies it. The ensureVoiceArmMutex race is the same shape A8 filed against bleCentralTxInit: two tasks reaching it first create two mutexes, one store wins, and since take and give both re-read the global rather than a captured handle, the give can target a different object than the take.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED — board: ENABLE_ESP_SR builds only — System_BuildConfig.h:293 sets it to 0, so NOT FeatherS3 today
- **Direction:** The fail-open branch is the substantive defect — a timeout on an arm/disarm should fail the operation, not perform it unlocked. Creating both mutexes at a single-threaded point in init removes the singleton race at the same time.

### [LOW] SR auto-tune mutates gSettings.srAfeGain and gSrAutoTuneStep from sr_task on core 1, bypassing setSetting — a gSettings writer not in the lifecycle audit's list
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/System_ESPSR.cpp:1426`
- **Also:** components/hardwareone/System_ESPSR.cpp:3495; callers at :2046 (srTask, core 1) and :3236 (cmd_exec_task, core 0)
- **What:** srAutoTuneCheck() does `gSrAutoTuneStep++` (:1409) and `gSettings.srAfeGain = cfg.afeGain;` (:1426) — a direct field write, not setSetting(), so it never marks settings dirty and never persists. The same function is reachable from two tasks: srTask (System_ESPSR.cpp:1917, pinned to core 1 at :2632, priority 5) calls it every telemetry period via srDebugPrintTelemetry at :2046, and cmd_sr_debug_stats (:3236) calls it from cmd_exec_task on core 0. cmd_sr_autotune at :3495 writes gSettings.srAfeGain directly as well.
- **Why it matters:** This answers the open question from SETTINGS_LIFECYCLE_AUDIT §4.5 about new writers: §4.5 enumerates cmd_exec (core 0), the unpinned g2_tap_disp worker, the OLED path on loopTask, boot-time writes, and MQTT/voice tasks. sr_task on core 1 is not among them, and it writes gSettings outside setSetting entirely. gSettings.srAfeGain is a 4-byte aligned float so there is no sub-word tearing and the settings serializer cannot observe a torn value; the concrete defects are (a) gSrAutoTuneStep++ is a non-atomic read-modify-write reachable from both cores, so running `sr debug stats` during an active auto-tune can skip or repeat a tuning step and index kAutoTuneConfigs inconsistently, and (b) the bypass of setSetting means the tuned gain silently reverts on the next settings write from any other path.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED — board: builds with ENABLE_ESP_SR only. Currently 0 (System_BuildConfig.h:293), so not active on FeatherS3.; prior art: EXTENDS docs/SETTINGS_LIFECYCLE_AUDIT.md §4.5 — the no-mutex finding stands unchanged (verified: zero settings-mutex symbols exist anywhere in the tree), and this is one NEW off-core writer the sectio
- **Direction:** Route the writes through setSetting so the value participates in the normal persistence and dirty-tracking discipline, and make auto-tune advancement single-owner — the step counter belongs to srTask, so the CLI entry point should read state rather than call the advancing function.

### [LOW] cmd_sensorinfo drives raw Wire with no bus mutex and then discards the result
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/System_I2C.cpp:1971`
- **What:** `cmd_sensorinfo` does `Wire.beginTransmission(addr); Wire.endTransmission()` directly (lines 1971-1972 and 1977-1978) to compute `connectedWire0`, then uses the manager-mediated `i2cPingAddress(...)` for `connectedWire1`. `Wire` is the manager's bus 1 (`wires[1] = &Wire`, System_I2C_Manager.cpp:53), so these probes bypass `busMutexes[1]` entirely. The result is then dead: the output block at lines 1986-1994 prints a ✓ line only for `connectedWire1`, and the ✗ line only when both are false — a device present solely on bus 1 produces no connection line at all.
- **Why it matters:** Two defects in one block. Functionally: on the FeatherS3[D], `sensorinfo ssd1306` (or any device actually on I2C2) prints the header "Connection Status:" followed by nothing, because `connectedWire0` is true and suppresses the ✗ branch while nothing prints the ✓ branch. Concurrency: the probe runs from cmd_exec_task with no bus-1 mutex, so it interleaves with the OLED render task's `displayUpdate()` sequence on the same bus. Individual Wire transactions stay atomic via TwoWire's internal lock, but a probe landing between the SSD1306's page-address command and its data payload corrupts that frame. The naming is also inverted relative to the manager's own convention — `Wire`/`connectedWire0` is bus 1 and the printed label says "Connected on Wire1".
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED — independently reported by 2 finders
- **Direction:** Replace both raw probes with `i2cPingAddress(addr, 100000, 50, 1)` and print a per-bus line ("I2C1: ✓ / I2C2: ✗") so both buses are actually reported. Then grep for any remaining bare `Wire.`/`Wire1.` outside a transaction lambda — after this and the camera probe, the only legitimate ones left are the boot warm-up at System_I2C.cpp:429 and `i2cSetDefaultWire1Clock()` at :3182.

### [LOW] The I2C CPU1-pinning rationale is written against the legacy I2C driver, which is not compiled on ESP-IDF 5.5.1
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/System_I2C_Manager.cpp:204`
- **Also:** scratchpad/recon_isr.md:52 and :230; components/arduino/cores/esp32/esp32-hal-i2c.c:19.
- **What:** The shared recon map (scratchpad/recon_isr.md line 52) attributes the I2C ISR to `i2c_driver_install(..., intr_alloc_flags=0)` at components/arduino/cores/esp32/esp32-hal-i2c.c:142. That entire file is wrapped in `#if ESP_IDF_VERSION < ESP_IDF_VERSION_VAL(5, 4, 0)` (line 19) and compiles to nothing on IDF 5.5.1. The active implementation is esp32-hal-i2c-ng.c -> i2c_new_master_bus -> IDF i2c_master.c:1076 esp_intr_alloc_intrstatus(..., I2C_INTR_ALLOC_FLAG, ...). sdkconfig has CONFIG_I2C_ISR_IRAM_SAFE unset and CONFIG_I2C_MASTER_ISR_HANDLER_IN_IRAM=y, so per i2c_private.h:57-61 the flag set is (SHARED | LOWMED) without ESP_INTR_FLAG_IRAM: the handler body is in IRAM but the interrupt is masked during flash operations. That is the safe configuration -- a stalled transaction times out rather than faulting -- which is the opposite of the crash risk the map's framing implies. The same stale premise appears in the in-code comment block at System_I2C_Manager.cpp:204-221, which explains beginBusOnCpu1 in terms of 'the legacy ESP-IDF I2C driver'.
- **Why it matters:** The technique still works — i2c_new_master_bus() also allocates its interrupt on the calling core, so running begin() on the CPU1 helper task still lands i2c_master_isr_handler_default on CPU1 — but the stated failure mode ("the legacy driver re-arms its command from inside the ISR") is a property of a driver that is not in the build and cannot be re-verified. Failure scenario: someone doing a cleanup pass reads :204, checks out esp32-hal-i2c.c, sees it is dead code on IDF 5.5, concludes the whole beginBusOnCpu1()/busBeginTask() apparatus is obsolete, and deletes it — reintroducing an Int-WDT-on-CPU0 exposure whose real current mechanism nobody has re-characterized. This is the only place first-party code deliberately controls interrupt core affinity, so it is worth having an accurate reason attached to it.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED — independently reported by 3 finders; board: all (bus 0 / Wire1 on every board that initializes it)
- **Direction:** Rewrite the rationale against the i2c_master driver (the argument is simply 'ISR affinity follows the installing core; keep it off the BLE controller's core'), and log a WARN in the `xTaskCreatePinnedToCore` failure branch so a degraded install is visible. Note for the ISR/IRAM track: the recon map's H1 row cites `esp32-hal-i2c.c:142` / `esp-idf/components/driver/i2c/i2c.c` as the ISR source, which is not compiled on IDF 5.5.1 — the real source is `esp_driver_i2c/i2c_master.c`, allocated with `I2C_INTR_ALLOC_FLAG` = `SHARED|LOWMED` (no `ESP_INTR_FLAG_IRAM`, because `CONFIG_I2C_ISR_IRAM_SAFE` is unset). The map's conclusion is right, its evidence path is wrong.

### [LOW] beginBusOnCpu1 waits on a completion semaphore with portMAX_DELAY while performBusRecovery holds the I2C bus mutex, and spawns at an undocumented priority 10
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/System_I2C_Manager.cpp:251`
- **Also:** components/arduino/libraries/Wire/src/Wire.cpp:287,383,412,423 (portMAX_DELAY on the TwoWire object lock); components/arduino/cores/esp32/esp32-hal-i2c-ng.c:87,177,249,303,351,396.
- **What:** beginBusOnCpu1 (:238-:253) creates the short-lived i2c0_begin task at priority 10 pinned to CPU1 and then blocks the caller on xSemaphoreTake(done, portMAX_DELAY) with no timeout. It is called from initBus (:284) at boot and from performBusRecovery (:402) — and performBusRecovery holds busMutexes[busIdx] across that call (taken at :363). The helper task's body calls wire->begin(), which itself takes TwoWire's internal lock with portMAX_DELAY (arduino/libraries/Wire/src/Wire.cpp:288). Priority 10 is outside the documented band (TASK_PRIORITY_LOW 1 / NORMAL 3 / HIGH 5, System_TaskUtils.h:124-127) and is unexplained at the site.
- **Why it matters:** Every wait in that chain is unbounded. If any task ever leaves a TwoWire transaction half-open (beginTransmission without a matching endTransmission/requestFrom, which holds the object lock and currentTaskHandle indefinitely), the recovery task blocks forever, the recovering task blocks forever on the semaphore while still holding the manager's bus mutex, and every other task on that bus then blocks on the bus mutex. The whole I2C subsystem deadlocks with no watchdog on the manager side. This is the one place in the manager where an unbounded wait is nested inside a held lock -- every other manager take is bounded (adaptiveTimeoutMs, 100 ms, 200 ms or 2000 ms).
- **Confidence:** PLAUSIBLE — independently reported by 2 finders; board: All boards with I2C bus 0
- **Direction:** Bound the wait (e.g. 2 s, matching the mutex acquire just above it); on expiry release the bus mutex and log a hard error rather than holding it forever. That requires moving BusBeginReq off the caller's stack — heap-allocate it and let the helper task free it after the give.

### [LOW] I2CDeviceManager::initialize()'s null-check on `new` is unreachable; the real OOM behaviour is a panic-reboot, not the wedge below it
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/System_I2C_Manager.cpp:57`
- **What:** `instance = new I2CDeviceManager(); if (!instance) { Serial.println("[I2C_MGR] FATAL: ..."); while(1) delay(1000); }`. CONFIG_COMPILER_CXX_EXCEPTIONS is not set (sdkconfig:730) and the link line pulls `-u __cxx_fatal_exception`, so a failing throwing `operator new` aborts rather than returning null.
- **Why it matters:** On genuine internal-DRAM exhaustion at I2C init the device panics and reboots (the crash record will capture it), it does not print the FATAL line and hang. The code reads as if it handles OOM gracefully at a site where three sibling wedges immediately below it (the mutex checks at :68-73 and :77-81) are reachable, which makes it easy to mistake the whole block for a tested path.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED — board: all (ENABLE_I2C_SYSTEM)
- **Direction:** Either use `new (std::nothrow)` so the check means something, or drop the check and note that OOM here reboots via abort. Low stakes either way — worth a line so the next reader is not misled about which of the four wedges in this function can actually fire.

### [LOW] After any `wire->end()`, a bypass caller caught between beginTransmission and endTransmission leaks TwoWire's internal lock permanently — the one true portMAX_DELAY hang in the I2C stack
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/System_I2C_Manager.cpp:371`
- **Also:** components/hardwareone/System_I2C.cpp:1971-1981, components/hardwareone/System_Camera_DVP.cpp:313-318
- **What:** `TwoWire::beginTransmission()` acquires the per-instance `lock` with `portMAX_DELAY` (Wire.cpp:423) and records `currentTaskHandle`; the lock is released only inside `endTransmission(true)` or `requestFrom()`. Both of those have an early-out that returns BEFORE the release: `endTransmission` returns 4 at Wire.cpp:452-455 when `txBuffer == NULL`, and `requestFrom` returns 0 at Wire.cpp:487-490 when either buffer is NULL. `TwoWire::end()` calls `freeWireBuffer()` (Wire.cpp:345), nulling both. `performBusRecovery` calls `wire->end()` at line 371 while holding only the manager's bus mutex — which the bypass callers (cmd_sensorinfo's raw `Wire` probes at System_I2C.cpp:1971-1981, the camera probe at System_Camera_DVP.cpp:313-314) do not take.
- **Why it matters:** If `performBusRecovery(1)` (e.g. from `oledResumeFromSleep`, OLED_Utils.cpp:6776) executes `wire->end()` in the window after a `sensorinfo` probe has called `Wire.beginTransmission()` but before its `Wire.endTransmission()`, the endTransmission takes the `txBuffer == NULL` early-out and returns 4 without giving the lock back. The mutex is now owned by cmd_exec_task with no release path. Every other task's next `beginTransmission` on that instance sees `currentTaskHandle != task` and blocks on `xSemaphoreTake(lock, portMAX_DELAY)` — forever. Bus 1 (the OLED on the primary board) is dead until reboot, with no timeout, no error, and no log line. This is the only unbounded wait left in the stack: the manager mutex, the wire-level transfer timeout, and the poll-pause counters are all bounded.
- **Confidence:** PLAUSIBLE — board: FeatherS3[D] (bus 1 has both a bypass caller and a live recovery trigger); the camera-probe variant is XIAO-only
- **Direction:** The window closes by itself if every caller goes through the manager, so the fix is the bypass-elimination work in the two findings above rather than patching vendored Wire.cpp. If belt-and-braces is wanted, have `performBusRecovery` blanket-`pollPause` and additionally take the target TwoWire's own lock (via a dummy zero-length `beginTransmission`/`endTransmission` pair) before calling `end()`. Marked PLAUSIBLE because it requires a specific interleaving I could not exercise on hardware; the code path itself is verified line by line.

### [LOW] The ps_alloc PSRAM-fallback diagnostic misses the exhausted-PSRAM case, its counter is never read, and the tagged ps_realloc overload omits the report entirely
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/System_MemUtil.h:134`
- **Also:** components/hardwareone/System_MemUtil.h:152 (tagged ps_alloc), :169 and :187 (both ps_calloc), :204 (untagged ps_realloc) share defect (a); :222-236 additionally has (c)
- **What:** Three defects in one mechanism whose stated purpose (header comment :9-16) is to make silent PSRAM->internal-DRAM redirection observable. (a) wantPS is computed as pref==PreferPSRAM && !psramBypassGlobal() && psramAvailableRuntime(); when psramAvailableRuntime() returns false the code falls straight through to malloc() and the guard 'if (p2 && wantPS) __psAllocReportFallback(...)' at :145/:163/:180/:198/:216 is skipped, because wantPS is already false. (b) gPsAllocFallbacks (:18) is incremented but has zero readers in the entire tree - no command, no diagnostic, no JSON field. (c) The tagged ps_realloc overload (:222-236) never calls __psAllocReportFallback at all, unlike its untagged twin at :203-219.
- **Why it matters:** (a) A caller that asks for PSRAM and receives internal DRAM instead is exactly the event this code exists to log, and the one path where PSRAM is fully depleted - the highest-consequence case, since large buffers then starve the ~300 KB internal heap - is the one path that produces neither the counter bump nor the ESP_LOGW line. The window is narrow (psramAvailableRuntime() only returns false at literally 0 free bytes; at 1..N free bytes heap_caps_malloc fails and the fallback is correctly reported), which is why this is LOW rather than higher. (b) makes the counter half of the mechanism dead weight - the header claims 'surfacing the count... turns that into a grep-able event' but nothing surfaces it. (c) means a tagged ps_realloc that falls back is invisible even in the normal case. Separately, 'gPsAllocFallbacks = gPsAllocFallbacks + 1' on a volatile is a non-atomic read-modify-write executed from many tasks, so concurrent fallbacks undercount.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED
- **Direction:** Compute wantPS from pref and psramBypassGlobal() only, and let the heap_caps_malloc attempt itself be the availability test - then the exhausted case reports like every other failure. Add the missing __psAllocReportFallback to the tagged ps_realloc, make the counter a __atomic_add_fetch, and expose it in the existing memory/heap diagnostic output so it is actually readable.

### [LOW] Every ps_alloc/ps_calloc/ps_realloc call pays 3 heap queries whose results are written to two globals nothing ever reads
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/System_MemUtil.h:32`
- **Also:** PsramJsonAllocator::allocate (System_MemUtil.h:289-296) does not call __capture_mem_before but does call psramAvailableRuntime() — one heap query per ArduinoJson pool block, on every PSRAM_JSON_DOC parse.
- **What:** __capture_mem_before() sets gAllocHeapBefore = ESP.getFreeHeap(), then calls ESP.getPsramSize() and conditionally ESP.getFreePsram(). It is invoked once or twice inside every one of the eight ps_alloc/ps_calloc/ps_realloc overloads. gAllocHeapBefore and gAllocPsBefore are defined at HardwareOne.cpp:190-191 and declared extern at System_MemUtil.h:29-30 — a whole-tree grep finds no read of either symbol anywhere in components/hardwareone/ or main/.
- **Why it matters:** 263 ps_alloc-family call sites pay this, including per-packet paths: System_ESPNow_Tx.cpp:253 and :283 allocate an AEAD payload copy per encrypted frame, System_ESPNow.cpp:2473 per TX, System_ESPNow_FsList.cpp:611 per remote FS_LIST request. Each of those calls now walks the registered-heap list three extra times for a value that is discarded. The individual cost is small (heap_caps_get_free_size/total_size are O(registered heaps) with an O(1) per-heap read), so this is waste rather than a hazard — but it is unconditional dead work on the firmware's radio hot path, and it makes ps_alloc look more expensive than it is to anyone profiling it.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED
- **Direction:** Delete __capture_mem_before() and the two globals, or gate the whole thing behind gAllocTrackerEnabled the way memAllocDebug already gates itself at HardwareOne.cpp:411.

### [LOW] [MEMSAMPLE] "Heap(all)" line pairs internal-only free/total/min with a MALLOC_CAP_8BIT largest block, so it can report a largest free block larger than the total heap
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/System_MemoryMonitor.cpp:166`
- **Also:** components/hardwareone/OLED_Mode_System.cpp:296 — `memoryRenderData.largestBlock = heap_caps_get_largest_free_block(MALLOC_CAP_8BIT)`. Harmless today only because the field is written and never rendered (verified: no other reference to largestBlock in that file), i.e. it is dead as well as wrong.
- **What:** `largestBlock = heap_caps_get_largest_free_block(MALLOC_CAP_8BIT)` matches both the internal and the SPIRAM heaps, while freeHeap/totalHeap/minFreeHeap on the same output line (:201-203) come from ESP.getFreeHeap/getHeapSize/getMinFreeHeap, all MALLOC_CAP_INTERNAL under arduino-esp32 3.x.
- **Why it matters:** The line prints as e.g. `Heap(all): 210/300 KB (30% used) | MinFree: 180 KB | Largest: 7100 KB` — the largest block reported is the PSRAM one, 20x the stated total. HardwareOne.cpp:1199 carries a comment recording that this exact bug ("MALLOC_CAP_8BIT alone was matching PSRAM and reporting ~1.9 MB as the DRAM largest block") was already found and fixed in heapLogSummary; the same mistake survives here and in the OLED path.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED
- **Direction:** Either qualify with MALLOC_CAP_INTERNAL|MALLOC_CAP_8BIT to match the rest of the line, or report the PSRAM largest block on the PSRAM line where it belongs. Delete the unused OLED field.

### [LOW] Internal-DRAM availability gates use free size only — there is no largest-free-block criterion anywhere in the memory-requirement registry, and one G2 viewer guard is inconsistent with its sibling
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/System_MemoryMonitor.cpp:113`
- **Also:** components/hardwareone/G2_Glasses.cpp:17580 (free-size guard) vs :17901 (largest-block guard). components/hardwareone/G2_Glasses.cpp:8136 sizes the heartbeat worker's stack adaptively from heap_caps_get_free_size and refuses below stack+1200 — same free-vs-contiguous mismatch, though it at least checks xTaskCreate's return at :8156.
- **What:** checkMemoryAvailable() compares ESP.getFreeHeap() against MemoryRequirement::minHeapBytes. The pool is correct (arduino-esp32 3.x getFreeHeap is MALLOC_CAP_INTERNAL) but the metric is not: a task stack is one unbroken block, and free size says nothing about contiguity. Twelve subsystems gate their start-up on this (System_ESPNow.cpp:10041, Bluetooth.cpp:901, and the ten i2csensor_*.cpp init paths).
- **Why it matters:** This is the literal P6 shape on a device whose own comments record 65-77% fragmentation: the gate can pass on 49,152 B free while the largest internal block is 9 KB, after which xTaskCreate fails anyway and the user gets a create-failure instead of the registry's clear "Insufficient heap: need NN KB" message. Today the minHeapBytes figures are ~4x over-conservative (the comment at :67-71 explains why), which masks the metric error — retuning them against real stack sizes, as that comment invites, would expose it. The same inconsistency shows inside G2: g2ShowMapPage (G2_Glasses.cpp:17901) correctly gates on heap_caps_get_largest_free_block(MALLOC_CAP_INTERNAL|MALLOC_CAP_8BIT) with an explicit comment about needing contiguity, while g2ShowBmpFile (G2_Glasses.cpp:17580) gates the same class of decode buffer — its own comment says "tens of KB" — on ESP.getFreeHeap() < 16*1024.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED
- **Direction:** Add a minLargestBlockBytes column to MemoryRequirement (set it to the task stack plus TCB) and check heap_caps_get_largest_free_block(MALLOC_CAP_INTERNAL|MALLOC_CAP_8BIT) alongside the free-size check; make g2ShowBmpFile use the same guard shape as g2ShowMapPage.

### [LOW] Two xTaskCreate call sites ignore the return value, leaving a wedged flag and a permanently half-initialised renderer
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/System_Microphone.cpp:1213`
- **What:** cmd_micviz sets gMicVisualizerRunning = true at :1207, calls xTaskCreatePinnedToCore at :1213 discarding the result, and unconditionally returns "Visualizer started". initAsyncMapRenderer (OLED_Mode_Map.cpp:925) likewise discards the mapRender create result at :949. Separately, initAsyncMapRenderer's guard is `if (sMapBufA) return;` at :926 while sMapBufA is assigned at :929, before sMapBufB and sMapShadeBuf are checked at :932 — so a partial PSRAM failure latches the guard.
- **Why it matters:** mic_viz: on create failure the CLI reports success, no visualizer runs, and gMicVisualizerRunning stays true; the next `micviz` takes the stop branch and reports "Stopping visualizer..." for a task that never existed, so the user needs two invocations to reach a usable state. mapRender: if either later ps_malloc fails, the function returns with sMapBufA allocated (and leaked), no semaphore, and no task — and because sMapBufA is now non-null every subsequent call is a no-op, so the async renderer is dead for the rest of the boot even after PSRAM frees up, leaving the map screen on a stale front buffer. Every other create site in the tree checks the return and rolls back.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED — board: mic_viz: ENABLE_MICROPHONE (live on FeatherS3 via G2 audio). mapRender: ENABLE_MAPS + OLED.; prior art: KNOWN in part — docs/PRE_1_0_CODE_HEALTH_AUDIT.md:1345 flags G2_Glasses.cpp's bare xTaskCreate calls for not using xTaskCreateLogged; those sites now all check pdPASS. These two are outside that doc's
- **Direction:** Check both returns; on failure clear gMicVisualizerRunning and return an error string. In initAsyncMapRenderer, allocate all three buffers before assigning sMapBufA (or use a separate sInitDone flag) so a partial failure can be retried instead of latching.

### [LOW] I2cLockGuard / i2cLock are dead but keep a non-reentrant portMAX_DELAY take on a non-recursive mutex, and always lock bus 0 regardless of target bus
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/System_Mutex.cpp:145`
- **Also:** System_Mutex.cpp:204-214 (i2cLock/i2cUnlock, same defaulted bus index), System_Mutex.cpp:74 + System_Mutex.h:300 (isI2cLockedByCurrentTask, defined for this guard and never called)
- **What:** I2cLockGuard's constructor does `xSemaphoreTake(mgr->getBusMutex(), portMAX_DELAY)` with no isHeldByCurrentTask() reentrancy check — the only guard in this file without one (FsLockGuard :86, MapCacheGuard :125, I2sMicLockGuard :163, JsonBufferGuard :222, MeshRetryGuard :243, FileTransferGuard :264, TopoStreamsGuard :285, EspNowTxGuard :309 all have it). The bus mutexes are plain xSemaphoreCreateMutex (System_I2C_Manager.cpp:69), not recursive. It also calls getBusMutex() with no argument, which defaults to busIdx = 0 (System_I2C_Manager.h:287), so it can only ever lock bus 0 no matter which bus the caller intends. I2cLockGuard, i2cLock(), i2cUnlock() and isI2cLockedByCurrentTask() have zero call sites in components/hardwareone/.
- **Why it matters:** No live defect today — nothing uses them. The trap is for the next user: any nested I2cLockGuard in one task self-deadlocks permanently on a portMAX_DELAY take, and any bus-1 caller silently serialises against bus-0 traffic instead of protecting its own bus, defeating the per-bus-mutex design that executeTransaction (System_I2C_Manager.h:323-345) implements correctly. The reentrancy convention established by the other eight guards makes the omission read like an oversight rather than a deliberate choice.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED
- **Direction:** Delete the four symbols — executeTransaction is the real, bounded-timeout locking path and every caller already goes through it. If they are kept for future use, add the isHeldByCurrentTask() early-return and make the bus index an explicit constructor parameter with no default.

### [LOW] Manual fsLock()/fsUnlock() is asymmetric with FsLockGuard and can release an outer guard's lock
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/System_Mutex.cpp:104`
- **Also:** Manual-pair sites: System_Settings.cpp (7), System_Debug.cpp (6), System_Battery.cpp (4), WebServer_Server.cpp (5), System_Maps.cpp (2), System_SensorLogging.cpp, System_User.cpp, WebPage_Maps.cpp
- **What:** `fsLock()` (:104) skips the take when the current task already holds gFsMutex, but `fsUnlock()` (:110) gives unconditionally whenever the current task holds it. So a manual fsLock/fsUnlock pair nested inside an outer `FsLockGuard` scope releases the OUTER lock at the inner unlock; the outer destructor then calls xSemaphoreGive on a mutex it no longer owns (which returns pdFALSE and does nothing, since the mutex count is already full). writeSettingsJson uses three such pairs (System_Settings.cpp:1019/1033, 1058/1075, 1088/1091) and is reachable from every `setSetting()` in the firmware (System_Settings.h:1147-1170).
- **Why it matters:** If any code path ever calls setSetting() — or batteryLogAppend, the System_Debug live-log writer, System_Maps track save, or the web file-view handler — while holding an FsLockGuard, filesystem mutual exclusion is silently lost for the remainder of the outer scope, allowing a concurrent writer to tear a read or interleave with a rename. I scanned every FsLockGuard scope in components/hardwareone for a nested manual-lock or setSetting call and found none, which matches the recon pass's result, so the hazard is currently latent rather than live.
- **Confidence:** PLAUSIBLE — prior art: recon_persist.md §2.2 / #11 raised it; I independently could not find a concrete nested caller either
- **Direction:** Make fsUnlock() a no-op unless the matching fsLock() actually performed the take (a per-task depth counter, or convert the remaining manual pairs to scoped guards). WebServer_Server.cpp:4315 also names a local `FsLockGuard fsLock`, shadowing the free function — worth renaming while you are there.

### [LOW] Sixteen live diagnostic log strings label byte-valued stack watermarks as "words" — the exact mechanism that produced this project's documented 4x sizing myth
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/System_TaskUtils.cpp:407`
- **Also:** components/hardwareone/System_I2C.h:226; components/hardwareone/System_ESPNow_Sensors.cpp:690; components/hardwareone/i2csensor_seesaw.cpp:439,480; i2csensor_mlx90640.cpp:1460,1501; i2csensor_bno055.cpp:1114,1155; i2csensor_vl53l4cx.cpp:710,750; i2csensor_apds9960.cpp:475,511; i2csensor_pa1010d.cpp:417,444; i2csensor_sths34pf80.cpp:553,579
- **What:** reportTaskStack() prints the same uxTaskGetStackHighWaterMark value twice — once correctly as "free=NNNB" and then as "watermark=NNNwords" (:400-407) — despite the comment directly above it (:377-379) stating the value is in bytes on this port. The same mislabel appears in 14 sensor-driver log strings (i2csensor_seesaw.cpp:439/:480, mlx90640:1460/:1501, bno055:1114/:1155, vl53l4cx:710/:750, apds9960:475/:511, pa1010d:417/:444, sths34pf80:553/:579) and in the auto-disable message in System_I2C.h:226 ("(%u/%u words free)", with parameters literally named totalStackWords/freeWords). System_ESPNow_Sensors.cpp:690 prints "stack free=%u words" with a comment immediately above admitting the label is wrong.
- **Why it matters:** The repo has already been burned once by exactly this: System_TaskUtils.h:23-27 documents that every historical KB figure and every cited HWM measurement in the sizing comments is 4x inflated because a reporter multiplied by 4, and recon_prior.md lists "*_STACK_WORDS as word counts" as refuted claim #15. The reporter arithmetic was fixed, but the operator-facing *labels* were not. Anyone sizing a stack from a live device log today reads "watermark=612 words", multiplies by 4, and concludes there is 2.4 KB of headroom where there is 612 bytes — reintroducing the same class of error the header exists to prevent. This is a diagnostic-integrity defect, not a runtime bug.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED — prior art: Related family called out in docs/ESPNOW_COMMENT_AUDIT.md §5 ("the 4x stack myth, third act"), which named Sensors.cpp:586 and Tx.cpp:107; the 14 sensor-driver sites and System_TaskUtils.cpp:407 are a
- **Direction:** Replace the literal "words" with "B" in these format strings and rename the freeWords/totalStackWords parameters in System_I2C.h's checkTaskStackSafety. A one-line change per site; the risk is entirely in leaving it.

### [LOW] xTaskCreateLogged defaults coreId to tskNO_AFFINITY, so omitting the argument silently violates the hard pinning rule
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/System_TaskUtils.h:188`
- **What:** The project's own logged-create wrapper declares `BaseType_t coreId = tskNO_AFFINITY` as a defaulted final parameter, and the comment at :180 documents that default. All twelve current callers (HardwareOne.cpp:1514, :1611; System_I2C.cpp:3052; System_ESPNow_Tx.cpp:156; System_TaskUtils.cpp:192, :233, :250, :268, :286, :305, :323, :341, :359) do pass a core, so the tree is compliant today.
- **Why it matters:** The one helper whose purpose is to standardise task creation is also the one whose default choice violates the policy. A future call such as xTaskCreateLogged(fn, "x", 4096, nullptr, TASK_PRIORITY_LOW, &h, "x") compiles cleanly and produces an unpinned task; if it touches I2C it reproduces the panic(4) bus-storm the policy exists to prevent, and nothing in the build or at runtime reports it.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED
- **Direction:** Make coreId a required parameter with no default. All twelve call sites already pass it, so the change is mechanical and converts the footgun into a compile error.

### [LOW] Boot anchor is only written on the NTP path, so users created during an RTC-only boot can never get a real creation timestamp
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/System_Utils.cpp:2483`
- **Also:** components/hardwareone/System_Debug.cpp:2309-2346 (the NTP-only gate), components/hardwareone/System_User.cpp:3327
- **What:** writeBootAnchor() (System_User.cpp:3327) has exactly one caller — System_Debug.cpp:2346, inside logTimeSyncedMarkerIfReady(), which is called only from the NTP-success branch at System_Utils.cpp:2418. The RTC fallback branch at :2483 calls resolvePendingUserCreationTimes() and notifyAutomationScheduler() but not logTimeSyncedMarkerIfReady(), so no anchor line is appended to /system/boot_anchors.json for that boot.
- **Why it matters:** Users created during a boot whose clock came from the DS3231 rather than NTP are persisted with `ntpAnchorId = gNTPAnchorId` (System_User.cpp:908/1417) and a boot-relative createdMs. resolvePendingUserCreationTimes() then finds no matching anchor (findMatchingAnchor at :3281) and falls through to approximateUserTimestamp() at :3308 — the account gets a synthesized ordinal date rather than its real creation time, permanently. Note the newer capture-file anchor system (System_TimeAnchors.cpp:384) gates on Clock::isSynced() and does handle the RTC-only case correctly, so the two parallel anchor systems disagree.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED — board: all; only reachable when the clock arrives from a non-NTP source (ENABLE_RTC_SENSOR builds, or `timeset`)
- **Direction:** Call logTimeSyncedMarkerIfReady() (or at minimum writeBootAnchor()) from the RTC fallback branch and from cmd_timeset, and gate it on Clock::isSynced() rather than `time(nullptr) > 0`, which is already true pre-sync.

### [LOW] FeatherS3 Arduino-variant SPI defaults (MOSI=35, SCK=36, MISO=37) are exactly the octal-PSRAM D6/D7/DQS pins, and System_VFS has a live SPI.begin() with no pin arguments
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/System_VFS.cpp:156`
- **Also:** components/arduino/variants/um_feathers3/pins_arduino.h:25-27; esp-idf soc/esp32s3/include/soc/spi_pins.h (MSPI_IOMUX_PIN_NUM_D6=35, D7=36, DQS=37)
- **What:** tryMountSD() has `#if defined(SD_SCK_PIN) && defined(SD_MISO_PIN) && defined(SD_MOSI_PIN) ... SPI.begin(SD_SCK_PIN, SD_MISO_PIN, SD_MOSI_PIN, SD_CS_PIN); #else SPI.begin(); #endif`. The bare SPI.begin() takes the active variant's MOSI/MISO/SCK. On um_feathers3 those are 35/37/36 — the pins consumed by octal flash/PSRAM on ESP32-S3.
- **Why it matters:** The #else is unreachable in the current board set (SD_CS_PIN is defined only in the XIAO Sense block, which also defines all three of SCK/MISO/MOSI). But the moment any board defines SD_CS_PIN without the other three — the obvious way to add SD to the FeatherS3 — the fallback grabs GPIO35/36/37. That is fine on the quad-PSRAM FeatherS3 and takes down the PSRAM bus on the octal XIAO, with a green build on both. This is precisely the 'works on one board, silently breaks the other' shape the pitfall calls out.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED — board: latent: FeatherS3 (quad, safe today) vs XIAO (octal, would break)
- **Direction:** Delete the bare SPI.begin() fallback and make the pin triple mandatory alongside SD_CS_PIN (a #error if SD_CS_PIN is defined without SD_SCK/MISO/MOSI), so a new board cannot inherit variant SPI defaults by accident.

### [LOW] `esp_wifi_init()`'s ESP_ERR_NO_MEM is captured and discarded at every call site — a WiFi-side internal-DRAM exhaustion presents as a 12-second "connection failed"
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/System_WiFi.cpp:1044`
- **Also:** System_WiFi.cpp:1153 (return fully discarded); the earlier :915-917 set_mode/start pair has the same shape. Overlaps A9/P41 (esp_err_t sweep) — filed here for the P6 angle specifically.
- **What:** `err = esp_wifi_init(&cfg);` followed only by `DEBUG_WIFIF("[connectWiFiIndex] esp_wifi_init() returned: %d", err);` — no branch on `err`. Execution falls straight into `esp_wifi_set_mode`, `esp_wifi_start`, `esp_wifi_set_config`, `esp_wifi_connect` (:1047-1060), each of which will fail against an uninitialised driver, each of which also only DEBUG-logs. The recovery path at :1152-1155 discards the return outright (`esp_wifi_init(&cfg);` with no assignment at all). `esp_wifi_init` is the canonical internal-only allocator on this chip: it claims the static RX/TX buffer pool (`CONFIG_ESP_WIFI_STATIC_RX_BUFFER_NUM=16`, `STATIC_TX_BUFFER_NUM=16`) and returns `ESP_ERR_NO_MEM` when internal DRAM cannot supply it.
- **Why it matters:** The scenario is BLE + G2 + ESP-NOW resident (the normal FeatherS3 state), internal DRAM tight, user issues a WiFi connect. `esp_wifi_init` returns `ESP_ERR_NO_MEM`; nothing checks it; the loop spins its full retry budget and reports `"Failed connecting to '<ssid>' after %d attempts - WiFi status: %d"` (:1160). The operator is told the AP is unreachable when the real cause is internal DRAM — the exact P6 "failure surfaces far from the cause" shape. The code even *knows* about this failure mode: the comment at :1108-1113 says retrying "ramps heap pressure — the next attempt's deinit/reinit can hit malloc-buffer-fail when BLE is also active", yet the one return value that would name it is thrown away. Contrast `initEspNow` (`System_ESPNow.cpp:9768-9800`), which switches on `ESP_ERR_NO_MEM` and reports "out of memory — free up DRAM and retry" with before/after heap figures.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED — board: all with ENABLE_WIFI
- **Direction:** Branch on `err` at :1044 and :1153, and translate `ESP_ERR_NO_MEM` into a user-visible reason the way `initEspNow` already does — that switch statement is the in-tree pattern to reuse rather than re-invent.

### [LOW] KNOWN, strengthened — the WiFi lastConnected field has zero readers anywhere in the tree, so the full settings.json rewrite it triggers on every successful connect is entirely wasted
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/System_WiFi.cpp:1099`
- **What:** connectWiFiIndex sets gWifiNetworks[i].lastConnected = millis() and immediately calls saveWiFiNetworks() (:1099-1100). The value is serialised (System_Settings.cpp:994) and deserialised (:1414, :1426). A tree-wide grep for `lastConnected` returns exactly five sites: the declaration (System_WiFi.h:17), the zero-init (System_WiFi.cpp:819), the write above, and the two settings serialise/deserialise lines. No status page, web API, CLI command or scheduler consumes it.
- **Why it matters:** Every (re)connect and every auto-reconnect performs a merge-read + full rebuild + tmp write + rename of settings.json — the largest state file on the device — purely to store a boot-relative millis() that nothing ever reads and that is meaningless across the reboot it survives. On a flaky AP this is the dominant settings.json write source. The prior audit framed this as a write-frequency problem; the added fact is that the field has no consumer at all, so the fix is a deletion rather than a redesign.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED — board: all (ENABLE_WIFI); prior art: docs/SETTINGS_LIFECYCLE_AUDIT.md §4.8
- **Direction:** Delete the lastConnected field and the saveWiFiNetworks() call at System_WiFi.cpp:1100.

### [NOTE] The crash hook adds ~264 B to the panic stack on exactly the crash class it was built to capture
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/System_CrashRecord.cpp:176`
- **What:** `crashRecordInstallPanicHook()` sets Arduino's `_panic_handler`, which makes `__wrap_esp_panic_handler` route through `handle_custom_backtrace` (esp32-hal-misc.c:361) *before* `__real_esp_panic_handler`. That function declares a full `arduino_panic_info_t p_info` on the panic stack -- `unsigned int backtrace[60]` = 240 B plus the scalar fields, ~264 B total (esp32-hal.h:153-161) -- and walks up to 100 frames to fill it. `crashPanicHook` then keeps only `CRASH_BT_MAX = 12` entries (:32, :139), so 192 B of that frame is written and discarded. Before `crashRecordInstallPanicHook()` runs at HardwareOne.cpp:1314, `_panic_handler` is NULL and none of this cost is paid.
- **Why it matters:** The headline case this feature exists for is a FreeRTOS stack-overflow abort (docs/CRASH_HISTORY_DESIGN.md:14-16), i.e. a panic where SP is already past the task's guard. Adding ~264 B of frame below an already-overflowed SP writes further into whatever is adjacent. The design is guarded against the worst outcome: `rtcPanicMagic = 0` is written first with an explicit barrier (:131-132) so a hook that faults mid-capture cannot leave a torn record that reads as authoritative, and IDF's `PANIC_ENTRY_COUNT_MAX = 2` gives exactly one retry before a hard restart. I could not construct a specific crash where the extra frame changes the outcome, and IDF's own panic printing uses the same stack -- so this is a cost/robustness note, not a demonstrated failure.
- **Confidence:** PLAUSIBLE
- **Direction:** If a cheaper capture is ever wanted, `esp_backtrace_get_start`/`get_next_frame` are unconditionally IRAM and could fill the 12 slots directly from a `__wrap_esp_panic_handler` this component owns, skipping Arduino's 60-entry intermediate. Not worth doing on this evidence alone -- worth knowing before anyone raises CRASH_BT_MAX.

### [NOTE] Shared recon map recon_isr.md is now stale: first-party IRAM_ATTR count is 1, not 0
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/System_CrashRecord.cpp:93`
- **What:** recon_isr.md section 0 states 'IRAM_ATTR / DRAM_ATTR: 0' for first-party code and builds its P1/P2 conclusion on that ('The firmware... defines ZERO IRAM-resident functions'). The new uncommitted file System_CrashRecord.cpp defines static void IRAM_ATTR crashCopyStr at :93, used by crashPanicHook (:163, :167) and by crashRecordBootConsume (:235-236).
- **Why it matters:** The map's headline is the premise several agents will lean on for P1/P2/P3. It is now off by one. I verified the specific P2 question and the answer is still clean: crashPanicHook touches only RTC_NOINIT_ATTR slow-RTC statics (:45-60), plain internal .bss statics (:63-78), g_panic_abort_details, and esp_app_get_elf_sha256_str() - no PSRAM, no heap, no flash op; and its own comment (:119-124) correctly records that panic_enable_cache() has already run. kPhaseNames (:80) is flash rodata but is only read from crashPhaseName(), which the panic hook does not call. So there is no defect here - only a correction to the map so nobody re-derives 'zero IRAM' as a standing invariant.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED
- **Direction:** No code change. Note in the map that System_CrashRecord.cpp (untracked, new) introduces the first first-party IRAM_ATTR function, and that it was checked clean for PSRAM/flash access.

### [NOTE] P8 measurement: 96,000 B of the 111 KB IRAM image sits in SRAM1 and is subtracted directly from the DRAM heap — none of it is first-party
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/System_CrashRecord.cpp:93`
- **Also:** CONFIG_ESP_WIFI_IRAM_OPT, EXTRA_IRAM_OPT, RX_IRAM_OPT, SLP_IRAM_OPT and LWIP_IRAM_OPTIMIZATION are all correctly left unset — those are the big-ticket IRAM consumers and this build already declines them.
- **What:** IRAM_ATTR count across components/hardwareone/ and main/: exactly ONE occurrence, `static void IRAM_ATTR crashCopyStr(...)` in the untracked System_CrashRecord.cpp. Measured from build/hardwareone-idf.map (2026-07-28 build): .iram0.vectors 1,028 B + .iram0.text 111,147 B; the image spans 0x40374000-0x4038F700 = 112,384 B, of which only the first 16,384 B lies in SRAM0 (IRAM-only). The remaining 96,000 B lies in SRAM1, the same silicon as the DRAM heap — proven by the linker output itself: .dram0.data starts at 0x3fc9f700, which is exactly the IRAM end 0x4038f700 minus the S3 alias offset 0x6F0000. Per-library IRAM bytes: freertos 18,531 · libbtdm_app 16,947 · esp_hw_support 16,238 · spi_flash 11,296 · hal 8,811 · heap 6,687 · libphy 5,029 · esp_ringbuf 4,265 · esp_system 3,998 · esp_driver_rmt 3,257 (109,913 B attributed).
- **Why it matters:** P8 asks whether IRAM growth is silently shrinking DRAM. The answer is that it is — 96 KB of it — but the firmware contributes zero of it, so there is no first-party regression risk to police and no reason to fear adding a genuinely-needed IRAM ISR. The reclaimable levers are all sdkconfig, currently at IDF defaults: CONFIG_SPI_FLASH_ROM_IMPL unset (≈11.3 KB), CONFIG_RINGBUF_PLACE_FUNCTIONS_INTO_FLASH unset (≈4.3 KB), CONFIG_HEAP_PLACE_FUNCTION_INTO_FLASH unset (≈6.7 KB). Together ≈22 KB, which on a ~250 KB internal heap is ~9% more DRAM and — more useful for P7 — moves the heap's low boundary 22 KB down, enlarging the contiguous region the G2 task stacks compete for. Related current statics for the budget: .dram0.data 25,736 B + .dram0.bss 55,384 B = 81,120 B (docs/HOT_PATH_HEAP_AUDIT_MECHANICS.md §1 says 99.9 KB — that figure is now 18.8 KB stale); .ext_ram.bss 289,484 B; heap starts at 0x3FCB33E0.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED — board: FeatherS3 / esp32s3 (measured from the current build; ESP32 secondary target not measured); prior art: docs/HOT_PATH_HEAP_AUDIT_MECHANICS.md §1 gives a partial static budget; it never measured IRAM and its .dram0 numbers predate the 2026-07-24 offload sweep.
- **Direction:** Treat this as a measurement, not a defect. If DRAM headroom is ever the binding constraint, evaluate CONFIG_SPI_FLASH_ROM_IMPL and CONFIG_RINGBUF_PLACE_FUNCTIONS_INTO_FLASH first — both are low-risk here since no first-party ISR is in IRAM. Leave CONFIG_FREERTOS_PLACE_FUNCTIONS_INTO_FLASH alone: IDF ISRs use FreeRTOS APIs during flash operations.

### [NOTE] getPsramBuffer<SIZE>() shares one static buffer across every call site with the same SIZE, while its tag parameter implies per-caller buffers
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/System_MemUtil.h:339`
- **Also:** PSRAM_STATIC_BUF (System_MemUtil.h:368) does not have the aliasing bug — it declares the static in the caller's scope — but shares the racy lazy-init.
- **What:** The template body declares `static char* buf = nullptr;` — one static per *template instantiation*, i.e. per SIZE, not per call site. getPsramBuffer<4096>("sddiag") and getPsramBuffer<4096>("anything-else") therefore return the same pointer, and the tag only affects which caller happened to allocate it first. The convenience wrappers getPsramBuffer1K/2K/4K make this trivially easy to hit. The documented usage example at :338 is exactly the misleading form.
- **Why it matters:** Two subsystems using the same size would silently share one buffer and corrupt each other's output. There are currently ZERO callers anywhere in components/hardwareone or main (verified by grep), so this is latent only. Secondary issue on the same lines: the `if (!buf) buf = ps_alloc(...)` lazy init is not thread-safe (buf is constant-initialised so there is no C++11 magic-static guard), so two tasks racing first use double-allocate and leak one block — the same shape appears in the ~30 in-tree `static char* jbuf; if (!jbuf) jbuf = ps_alloc(...)` command buffers, which are safe today only because commands are serialized on cmd_exec and esp_http_server runs a single instance (only one of httpd_start / httpd_ssl_start is ever reached — WebServer_Server.cpp:5237 vs :5309).
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED
- **Direction:** Either delete getPsramBuffer/1K/2K/4K as dead scaffolding, or make the identity per-call-site (e.g. a tag template parameter, or require the caller to own the static) so the tag argument cannot lie.

### [NOTE] Tagged ps_realloc overload never reports a PSRAM->internal fallback, blinding gPsAllocFallbacks exactly where a tag exists
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/System_MemUtil.h:233`
- **Also:** System_MemUtil.h:289-296 (PsramJsonAllocator, no accounting at all — not previously reported); System_MemUtil.h:18 (counter never read).
- **What:** `inline void* ps_realloc(void* ptr, size_t size, AllocPref pref, const char* tag)` falls through to `void* p2 = realloc(ptr, size);` at :233 and goes straight to `__memAllocReport(...)` at :234. Its five siblings all carry `if (p2 && wantPS) __psAllocReportFallback(size, tag);` first — `ps_alloc` :145 and :163, `ps_calloc` :180 and :198, untagged `ps_realloc` :216. Verified by reading all six.
- **Why it matters:** `gPsAllocFallbacks` is the only signal in the firmware that distinguishes "PSRAM was requested and internal DRAM was silently handed over instead" from a normal allocation — a fallback is both a byte hit and a fragmentation hit, since a buffer sized for PSRAM lands in a ~300 KB internal heap. A tagged `ps_realloc` that falls back is invisible to that counter and to the `ESP_LOGW` beside it. Compounding it: `gPsAllocFallbacks` itself is read by nothing — `grep` across `components/` and `main/` finds only its definition (:18) and its increment (:21). And `PsramJsonAllocator::allocate()` (:289-296), which backs every `PSRAM_JSON_DOC` in the tree, falls back to bare `malloc()` with no counter and no log at all; its `psramAvailableRuntime()` gate (:120-131) returns true on `freePs > 0`, so a nearly-full PSRAM routes every JsonDocument into internal DRAM silently.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED — independently reported by 2 finders; prior art: KNOWN — docs/HOT_PATH_HEAP_AUDIT_MECHANICS.md, "Diagnostics gaps": "the tagged ps_realloc overload (System_MemUtil.h, last of four) is missing the __psAllocReportFallback() call its three siblings hav
- **Direction:** Add the missing call so all six wrappers agree, and consider routing `PsramJsonAllocator`'s fallback through the same counter. Surfacing `gPsAllocFallbacks` in the `[MEMSAMPLE]` line would make the whole diagnostic reachable — right now it is write-only.

### [NOTE] System_NeoPixel's NEOPIXEL_I2C_POWER legacy fallback is unreachable
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/System_NeoPixel.cpp:146`
- **Also:** components/hardwareone/System_BuildConfig.h:780, :800, :823, :864, :904, :968, :987 (every board block defines NEOPIXEL_POWER_PIN)
- **What:** `#if defined(NEOPIXEL_I2C_POWER) && !defined(NEOPIXEL_POWER_PIN)` can never be true — every branch of the board chain, including the unsupported #else, defines NEOPIXEL_POWER_PIN (as -1 where absent). The Adafruit Feather V2 variant does define NEOPIXEL_I2C_POWER=2, but the board block already maps it to NEOPIXEL_POWER_PIN 2, so the fallback never fires.
- **Why it matters:** Harmless today, but it reads as a working safety net for boards that only expose the variant's NEOPIXEL_I2C_POWER. A new board added without an explicit NEOPIXEL_POWER_PIN would fall into the #else (which defines it as -1), so the rail would silently never be asserted rather than being picked up by this branch. On a Feather-V2-class board that rail also powers the STEMMA QT connector, so the failure would present as 'no I2C devices'.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED
- **Direction:** Delete the dead branch, or change the condition to `#if defined(NEOPIXEL_I2C_POWER) && NEOPIXEL_POWER_PIN < 0` so it actually covers the case it was written for.

### [NOTE] Fix-verification: HOT_PATH_HEAP_AUDIT's #1 finding is still unfixed — `readText()` still reads every file one byte at a time
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/System_Utils.cpp:806`
- **Also:** Same `Stream::readString` inheritance applies to any other `f.readString()` in the tree.
