- **What:** `heap_caps_register_failed_alloc_callback` has zero occurrences in components/hardwareone/ or main/. sdkconfig has CONFIG_HEAP_ABORT_WHEN_ALLOCATION_FAILS unset, CONFIG_HEAP_USE_HOOKS unset, CONFIG_HEAP_TRACING_OFF=y, CONFIG_HEAP_POISONING_DISABLED=y, CONFIG_HEAP_TASK_TRACKING unset. IDF's heap_caps_alloc_failed() therefore does nothing on every failed malloc. Independently, the vendored Arduino String turns a failed allocation into silence: String::copy (WString.cpp:234-242) calls invalidate() when reserve() fails, and invalidate() (WString.cpp:165-170) resets the object to an empty SSO string.
- **Why it matters:** A device running a ~250 KB internal heap that its own comments describe as 65-77% fragmented has no way to observe an OOM. An internal-DRAM failure inside WiFi, Bluedroid, lwIP, ArduinoJson or any String assignment produces either a silently truncated result, a silently empty command output, or a distant NULL-deref crash with no trail back to the cause — the exact P6 signature ("the failure surfaces far from the cause"). This is the cheapest available instrumentation: the callback costs nothing when no failure occurs and yields size + caps + the failing function name.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED — prior art: The gPsAllocFallbacks surfacing gap and the tagged-ps_realloc hole are KNOWN — see docs/HOT_PATH_HEAP_AUDIT_MECHANICS.md §3; both re-verified unchanged. The failed-alloc callback is not mentioned in a
- **Direction:** Register a heap_caps_register_failed_alloc_callback in setup() that logs size, caps and the requesting task, and add gPsAllocFallbacks to the periodic [MEMSAMPLE] line while you are there.

### [LOW] Camera and PDM-mic interrupts are allocated without ESP_INTR_FLAG_IRAM, so every flash operation masks them — bounded loss on XIAO builds
- **Where:** `managed_components/espressif__esp32-camera/target/esp32s3/ll_cam.c:418`
- **Also:** components/hardwareone/HAL_Audio.cpp:56-58 (I2S PDM channel config); sdkconfig:2804 (# CONFIG_LCD_CAM_ISR_IRAM_SAFE not set), sdkconfig:1298 (# CONFIG_I2S_ISR_IRAM_SAFE not set); write path that triggers it: System_ImageManager.cpp:226/240/261/276.
- **What:** esp32-camera registers its GDMA in_suc_eof and LCD_CAM vsync ISRs with CAMERA_ISR_IRAM_FLAG, which expands to 0 because CONFIG_LCD_CAM_ISR_IRAM_SAFE is not set; the I2S PDM channel is created without CONFIG_I2S_ISR_IRAM_SAFE. Neither handler is IRAM-resident. esp_intr_noniram_disable() masks both for the duration of every flash op on both cores.
- **Why it matters:** This is the benign half of P1: because the flag is absent these ISRs are masked rather than faulted, so there is no Cache Error panic — the cost is missed interrupts. Concretely, saving a JPEG to LittleFS is one blocking f.write of the whole frame (System_ImageManager.cpp:226), so a 100 KB capture is ~25 sector erases plus ~200 program bursts ≈ 1 s during which the camera's VSYNC/EOF ISRs cannot run; esp32-camera reports that as the `cam_hal: FB-OVF` / `EV-VSYNC-OVF` bursts at cam_hal.c:256-299, which System_Debug.cpp:287-292 already cites by name as an observed real-world flood. The PDM mic is comfortably covered for a single erase — dma_desc_num=4 x dma_frame_num=1024 at AUDIO_HAL_SAMPLE_RATE 16000 is 256 ms of DMA buffering versus a 25-50 ms erase — but not for a multi-second log rotation. No first-party ISR is affected: components/hardwareone and main contain zero IRAM_ATTR/DRAM_ATTR symbols and register zero interrupt handlers.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED — board: XIAO S3 Sense only (ENABLE_CAMERA_SENSOR / ENABLE_MICROPHONE_SENSOR; both are 0 on the checked-in FeatherS3 build)
- **Direction:** Nothing to fix in first-party code. If XIAO camera builds start dropping frames during captures, the levers are (a) route captures to SD rather than LittleFS while streaming, or (b) chunk the JPEG write so the driver gets scheduling gaps — not enabling the ISR_IRAM_SAFE options, which would cost IRAM this build does not have (MEMORY_LAYOUT.md notes IRAM near full with BT on).

### [NOTE] CMakeLists comment describes a `__wrap_esp_panic_handler` that System_CrashRecord.cpp does not define
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/CMakeLists.txt:463`
- **What:** The new comment block says "System_CrashRecord.cpp defines `__wrap_esp_panic_handler()`, stashes the crash detail in RTC memory, and then tail-calls `__real_esp_panic_handler()`". It does not. The file registers a callback via Arduino's `set_arduino_panic_handler(crashPanicHook, nullptr)` (System_CrashRecord.cpp:175-177); the wrap symbol is owned by components/arduino/cores/esp32/esp32-hal-misc.c:418. System_CrashRecord.h:65-73 explains this correctly, including why a second definition would be a duplicate-symbol link error.
- **Why it matters:** Anyone tracing the panic path from the build file will grep System_CrashRecord.cpp for `__wrap_esp_panic_handler`, find nothing, and conclude the interposition is broken — when in fact it works (the flag is on the final link line in build/build.ninja, and `nm build/hardwareone-idf.elf` shows both `__wrap_esp_panic_handler` and `crashPanicHook`). The two comments in the same change contradict each other, and the wrong one is the one a reader hits first.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED
- **Direction:** Point the CMake comment at Arduino's wrapper and at `set_arduino_panic_handler`, matching the header's account. One or two lines.

### [NOTE] Recon map correction: recon_tasks.md lists a `loopTask` that does not exist in this build
- **Where:** `main/hardwareone-idf.cpp:26`
- **Also:** scratchpad/recon_tasks.md §2 and §6
- **What:** `recon_tasks.md` §6 lists `loopTask` — 8192 B, priority 1, Core 1 (`CONFIG_ARDUINO_RUNNING_CORE=1`) — as a live platform task, and §2 reasons about `loopTaskWDTEnabled = false` making its `esp_task_wdt_reset()` dead. Neither applies: `# CONFIG_AUTOSTART_ARDUINO is not set` (sdkconfig:603) and `components/arduino/cores/esp32/main.cpp:25` wraps both `loopTask` and Arduino's `app_main` in `#if CONFIG_AUTOSTART_ARDUINO`, so that whole file compiles to nothing here. `main/hardwareone-idf.cpp:26-53` supplies its own app_main. setup()/loop() therefore run on the IDF `main` task: 8192 B (`CONFIG_ESP_MAIN_TASK_STACK_SIZE`), Core 0 (`CONFIG_ESP_MAIN_TASK_AFFINITY_CPU0=y`, sdkconfig:1773), priority 1, never deleted because app_main never returns.
- **Why it matters:** The conclusion the map is used for flips: the OLED refresh, power-save/CPU-clock switching, automation dispatch, MQTT tick, battery sampling, event drain and serial CLI all live on Core 0 (the radio core), not Core 1. `CONFIG_ARDUINO_RUNNING_CORE=1` is still present in sdkconfig and is what makes the wrong reading look verified — it is dead config in an AUTOSTART-off build. Measured frames for the real task: hardwareone_setup() 544 B (`entry a1, 0x220`), hardwareone_loop() 560 B (`entry a1, 0x230`), app_main 240 B, from the shipped ELF. The map's §2 TWDT conclusion still holds for the right reason: `esp_task_wdt_*` has 0 occurrences in components/hardwareone and main, and `CONFIG_ESP_TASK_WDT_PANIC` is not set, so IDLE0/IDLE1 remain the only subscribers.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED — board: all (sdkconfig.esp32:392 and :1381 match)
- **Direction:** Any other agent reasoning about Core 0 saturation, Core 1 headroom, or which task owns the OLED/I2C should re-read that row as `main` on Core 0. Worth flagging to whoever owns the task inventory.


### OLED UI — 10 findings (3 critical/high)

### [HIGH] Location-context bar overflows a 128-byte stack array when map road + area names are long
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/OLED_Mode_Map.cpp:1230`
- **Also:** OLED_Mode_Map.cpp:1218, 1223, 1225 are the same chain; 1230 is the one that actually lands out of bounds.
- **What:** `char contextBuf[128]; int pos = 0;` then three chained `pos += snprintf(contextBuf + pos, sizeof(contextBuf) - pos, ...)` calls with no clamp between them. `sizeof(contextBuf) - pos` is unsigned arithmetic against a signed `pos`, so once `pos` exceeds 128 the size argument wraps to ~4.29e9 and `contextBuf + pos` is already past the array.
- **Why it matters:** `LocationContext::nearestRoad` and `nearestArea` are `char[64]` (System_Maps.h:527,531), each filled by `strncpy(..., map.names[nameIndex].name, 63)` in `LocationContextManager::updateContext` (System_Maps.cpp:3886) from the map file's name table (`char name[64]`, System_Maps.h:327). With a 63-char road and a 63-char area: first call returns 3+63=66; second returns 10+63=73 (the ' • ' bullet is 3 UTF-8 bytes) so `pos` = 139 > 128; the third call (`areaDistanceM < 500.0f`) then writes ~8 bytes at contextBuf[139..146] — 11–18 bytes past a 128-byte array on `loopTask`'s 8192-byte stack, since `displayGPSMap` runs from `oledUpdate()` in the main loop. Map files are user-supplied (uploadable via the web file API), so the trigger is attacker-choosable, not just unlucky OSM data.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED — board: boards building ENABLE_MAPS + OLED display (FeatherS3 OLED config)
- **Direction:** Clamp after each append (`if (pos >= (int)sizeof(contextBuf)) pos = sizeof(contextBuf)-1;`) or guard each call with `if (pos < (int)sizeof(contextBuf) - 1)`. The same fix pattern is already used correctly in G2_Page_Sensors.cpp:858/874.

### [HIGH] OLED map context bar overflows a 128-byte stack buffer when road + area names are long (snprintf return accumulated with no clamp)
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/OLED_Mode_Map.cpp:1223`
- **What:** `char contextBuf[128]; int pos = 0;` then three chained `pos += snprintf(contextBuf + pos, sizeof(contextBuf) - pos, ...)` calls at :1218, :1223/:1225 and :1230. `sizeof(contextBuf)` is size_t and `pos` is int, so once `pos` exceeds 128 the size argument wraps to ~4.29e9 and the destination pointer is already past the array. LocationContext::nearestRoad and ::nearestArea are both char[64] (System_Maps.h:527/531), filled by strncpy from MapNameEntry::name[64] read out of a .map file (System_Maps.cpp:3889/3901, load at :495).
- **Why it matters:** A map file whose nearest road name is 63 chars and nearest area name is 63 chars: call 1 writes "On " + 63 = 66 (fits, pos=66); call 2 wants " • Near " + 63 = ~73 but only 62 bytes remain, so it truncates and returns 73 -> pos = 139; call 3 (taken whenever ctx.areaDistanceM < 500.0f) becomes `snprintf(contextBuf + 139, (size_t)(128 - 139), " (%.0fm)", ...)` and writes 8 bytes at offsets 139..146 — 11 to 19 bytes past the end of the automatic array in displayGPSMap(), on the OLED render task's stack. Combined name length only has to exceed ~115 chars, which real OSM road+area names reach.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED — board: boards with ENABLE_OLED_DISPLAY && ENABLE_MAPS && ENABLE_GPS_SENSOR (I2C_FEATURE_LEVEL 3 — FeatherS3 primary); compiled out at levels 0-2
- **Direction:** Clamp after each call (`if (pos < 0 || pos >= (int)sizeof(contextBuf)) pos = (int)sizeof(contextBuf) - 1;`) or bound each field in the format (`%.40s`). Making `pos` a size_t would not help — it makes the underflow silent instead of merely unsignedy.

### [HIGH] Four OLED code paths use the bus-0-hardcoded transaction helper while the OLED lives on bus 1 by default on the primary board — wrong mutex, wrong bus clocked, phantom health entry
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/OLED_Utils.cpp:4687`
- **Also:** components/hardwareone/OLED_Utils.cpp:3527, :4131, :4191
- **What:** `i2cOledTransactionVoid(clockHz, timeoutMs, op)` (System_I2C.h:160) forwards to the 4-argument `i2cDeviceTransactionVoid(I2C_ADDR_OLED, ...)`, whose `busIdx` defaults to 0. Four live sites still call it: OLED_Utils.cpp:3527 (`stopOLEDDisplay`), :4131 (`oledmode off`), :4191 (`oledclear`), :4687 (boot animation's first frame). On the FeatherS3[D] `gSettings.oledBus` defaults to 1 (`OLED_BUS_DEFAULT`, System_BuildConfig.h:1088-1094, System_Settings.h:246) and `i2c2Enabled` defaults to 1. The SSD1306 object was constructed with bus 1's `TwoWire*` (HAL_Display.cpp:64 / OLED_Utils.cpp:4651), so the actual bytes go out on `Wire`/bus 1 while the manager locks `busMutexes[0]` and calls `setBusClock(0, 400000)` on `Wire1`.
- **Why it matters:** Concrete failure on the primary board: (a) the display write is unsynchronised against bus 1 — the render path `displayUpdate()` (HAL_Display.cpp:173) correctly takes `busMutexes[1]`, so a `oledclear` on cmd_exec_task and a frame push on the render task can interleave their SSD1306 page-address command sequences (each individual Wire transaction is still atomic via TwoWire's own lock, but the multi-transaction sequence is not) → garbled/half-drawn frames. (b) `Wire1` — the primary sensor bus, carrying the gamepad — is clocked to 400 kHz and its mutex held for the duration of a full framebuffer push, blocking real bus-0 devices for nothing. (c) The frame is pushed at bus 1's *current* clock (100 kHz), not the intended 400 kHz, so it takes ~4x longer than the code assumes. (d) Because these are the `void` overload, `executeTransaction` unconditionally calls `recordSuccess()`, so a phantom `0x3D "Auto"` device appears on bus 0 in `i2chealth`/`i2cscan` with a live `lastSuccessTime`, inflating `getActiveDeviceCount()`. Site :4687 runs on every boot.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED — board: FeatherS3[D] (ARDUINO_UM_FEATHERS3_DEV) — the only board where OLED_BUS_DEFAULT is 1; benign on boards where oledBus==0
- **Direction:** Replace all four with the bus-aware form already used everywhere else in the same file: `i2cDeviceTransactionVoid((uint8_t)gSettings.oledBus, I2C_ADDR_OLED, 400000, 500, ...)`. Then delete `i2cOledTransactionVoid`/`i2cOledTransaction` from System_I2C.h so no new caller can reintroduce the bus-0 assumption.

### [MEDIUM] OLED notification banner queue and pairing ribbon are mutated cross-core with no lock, in the same file where the sibling toast primitive is spinlock-protected
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/OLED_UI.cpp:526`
- **Also:** components/hardwareone/OLED_UI.cpp:544 (pump), :554 (oledPairingRibbonShow), :746 (oledNotificationBannerUpdate, same unguarded struct write); components/hardwareone/System_ESPNow.cpp:14383 (cross-core producer); components/hardwareone/i2csensor_rda5807.cpp:698; components/hardwareone/System_ESPSR.cpp:1010, :1091
- **What:** `oledBannerQueuePush()` (:526) and `oledBannerQueuePump()` (:544) both read-modify-write `sBannerQHead` and `sBannerQCount` (plain `static uint8_t`, :522-523) with no synchronization of any kind, and push does a 128-byte `strncpy` into `sBannerQueue[tail]` while pump reads `sBannerQueue[sBannerQHead]`. `oledPairingRibbonShow()` (:554) writes the whole `gOledPairingRibbon` struct field-by-field — `message[]` via strncpy, then icon, visibleDurationMs, iconBlink, blinkCount, state, stateStartMs, animY, scrollOffset, lastScrollMs. The pump and the animation state machine (`oledPairingRibbonUpdate`, :600) and the renderer (`oledPairingRibbonRender`, :785) all run on loopTask (APP_CORE/core 1) via `oledUIRender()` (:883). The producers do not: `cmd_espnowremote` calls `oledNotificationBannerShow()` at System_ESPNow.cpp:14383 on cmd_exec_task (PRO_CORE/core 0), and `i2csensor_rda5807.cpp:698` calls it from fmradio_task. 350 lines earlier in this same file, `oledToastShow/Clear/Active` (:152-181) protect an equivalent structure with `sToastMux`, so the protection is present for the toast and absent for the banner.
- **Why it matters:** Issue `espnowremote <mac> <cmd>` from serial/web/BLE while a notification banner is animating on the OLED. cmd_exec on core 0 executes `sBannerQCount++` while loopTask on core 1 executes `sBannerQCount--`; one update is lost. A lost decrement leaves a count higher than the number of real entries, so the pump replays a zero-initialized BSS slot as an empty banner and burns the ribbon's animation window on nothing; a lost increment silently drops the notification. Concurrently, core 0's `strncpy` into `gOledPairingRibbon.message` races core 1's `oledPairingRibbonRender()` reading that same buffer, and `state = UNFURLING` can be observed with a stale `stateStartMs`, making `elapsed` a huge value that skips the animation. Display corruption and lost notifications, not memory unsafety — the ring index is always taken modulo BANNER_QUEUE_MAX.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED — board: all builds with ENABLE_OLED_DISPLAY
- **Direction:** The banner queue wants the same treatment the toast already has in this file — a short portMUX around the head/count/entry mutation on both the push and pump sides. Whether the ribbon struct itself needs the same gate depends on whether producers should be allowed to bypass the queue at all when the ribbon is idle.

### [MEDIUM] oledDirtyUntilMs uses a monotonic max plus an absolute millis() compare, so a millis() rollover pins the OLED permanently dirty for the next 49.7 days
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/OLED_Utils.cpp:3065`
- **Also:** components/hardwareone/OLED_Utils.cpp:3076; the same absolute-deadline shape governs the toast at OLED_UI.cpp:175
- **What:** oledMarkDirtyUntil() only ever raises the deadline (`if (untilMs > oledDirtyUntilMs) oledDirtyUntilMs = untilMs;`) and oledIsDirty() tests `millis() < oledDirtyUntilMs` (:3076). Both are plain absolute comparisons on a wrapping 32-bit counter.
- **Why it matters:** If any timed popup is armed shortly before the 49.7-day millis() wrap, oledDirtyUntilMs holds a near-0xFFFFFFFF value. After the wrap millis() restarts near 0, so (a) `millis() < oledDirtyUntilMs` is true continuously for the next ~49.7 days and (b) every subsequent oledMarkDirtyUntil() call passes a smaller value and is discarded, so the state can never be cleared. oledIsDirty() is the gate that skips the expensive render at OLED_Utils.cpp:3668, so the device drops out of dirty-render mode entirely and performs a full frame build plus I2C flush at gSettings.oledUpdateInterval (default 125 ms) forever. Continuous CPU, I2C-bus and battery cost with no user-visible cause. Callers that arm it exist in seven UI modes (OLED_Mode_Auth, OLED_Mode_ChangePassword, OLED_Mode_UserManager, OLED_Mode_Automations, OLED_Mode_System, OLED_ESPNow).
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED — board: any build with ENABLE_OLED_DISPLAY
- **Direction:** Store the deadline and compare with the signed-difference idiom already used elsewhere in this tree — `(int32_t)(untilMs - oledDirtyUntilMs) > 0` for the raise and `(int32_t)(oledDirtyUntilMs - millis()) > 0` for the test (see System_ESPNow.cpp:4641 and System_ESPNow_FsList.cpp:923 for the in-repo precedent).

### [LOW] OLED memory screen walks every free block in both heaps on each refresh and throws the result away — and would show PSRAM's largest block if it did render it
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/OLED_Mode_System.cpp:296`
- **Also:** Same wrong-cap pattern, but there the value is genuinely used: System_MemoryMonitor.cpp:166 (`largestBlock` for the "Heap(all)" line — consistent with that line's own scope, so arguably fine).
- **What:** `memoryRenderData.largestBlock = heap_caps_get_largest_free_block(MALLOC_CAP_8BIT);` in `prepareMemoryData()`. `memoryRenderData.largestBlock` and `.minFreeHeap` (:295) are written here and read nowhere — `displayMemoryStatsRendered()` (:302-364) renders only the heap and PSRAM free/total bars. Two separate problems in one line: (a) the value is dead, and (b) the cap is wrong. `MALLOC_CAP_8BIT` alone matches PSRAM too, so on this hardware the number is PSRAM's largest block (megabytes), sitting in a struct field next to internal-DRAM totals. `HardwareOne.cpp:1196-1198` carries a comment recording that this exact bug was found and fixed there once: "MALLOC_CAP_8BIT alone matches PSRAM too; restrict to internal so this reports the actual DRAM largest free block (was returning ~1.9 MB PSRAM)." That fix never reached this site.
- **Why it matters:** `heap_caps_get_largest_free_block` is O(free blocks) across every registered heap and takes the heap lock for the walk. `prepareMemoryData()` runs on each refresh of the OLED memory screen, in the main-loop render path, deliberately positioned "OUTSIDE I2C transaction" (:276) to avoid blocking the gamepad — so someone already cared about this function's cost. It is paying that walk for a value nothing consumes. The user-visible consequence of the dead field is that the OLED memory screen — the one memory surface reachable without a serial console — shows no fragmentation information at all, and the field that was clearly meant to provide it would have shown the wrong pool.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED — board: all with ENABLE_OLED_DISPLAY
- **Direction:** Either drop the two dead assignments, or render them and change the cap to `MALLOC_CAP_INTERNAL | MALLOC_CAP_8BIT` to match `heapLogSummary` at HardwareOne.cpp:1198 — that comment is the record of why.

### [LOW] OLEDConsoleBuffer::append() takes the mutex but getLine()/getLineCount()/getTimestamp() take nothing, and getLine() returns a raw pointer into the live ring
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/OLED_Utils.cpp:2518`
- **Also:** components/hardwareone/OLED_Utils.cpp:2513, :2537 (the other two unlocked accessors); OLED_ConsoleBuffer.h:53-56 (declarations)
- **What:** `append()` (:2464) correctly takes `mutex` with a 10 ms timeout before writing `lines[head]`, `timestamps[head]`, `head` and `count`. The three accessors — `getLineCount()` (:2513), `getLine()` (:2518), `getTimestamp()` (:2537) — are `const` and take no lock at all; they read `count`, `head` and `capacity` unsynchronized and `getLine()` hands the caller `lines[bufferIndex]`, a bare pointer into the ring slot that append() may be mid-way through overwriting. The producer is `debugOutputTask` (`debug_out`, PRO_CORE/core 0 — the class comment at :2407 confirms it is running concurrently). The consumers are the OLED CLI console screens on loopTask (APP_CORE/core 1).
- **Why it matters:** Any debug or command output arriving while the OLED console screen is rendering: core 0 is inside append()'s character-filter loop writing `dst[dstIdx++]` one byte at a time into the exact slot core 1 is printing, so the rendered line is a splice of the old and new text. Because the ring is documented as routinely holding echoed `login <user> <pass>` plaintext, a spliced line can surface a fragment of one entry inside another. Indexing stays in bounds (`% capacity`) and the 64-byte slot always retains a NUL, so this is display corruption rather than memory unsafety.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED — board: all builds with ENABLE_OLED_DISPLAY
- **Direction:** An accessor that returns an interior pointer cannot be made safe by locking inside itself — the read wants either a copy-out API under the same mutex, or the whole render pass wrapped in one acquisition.

### [LOW] FeatherS3 GPIO39 powers both the RGB LED and the I2C2 rail; sleep drops it and wake restores only the OLED, leaving the LED dark
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/OLED_Utils.cpp:6724`
- **Also:** components/hardwareone/OLED_Utils.cpp:6745-6756 (oledResumeFromSleep), components/hardwareone/System_Utils.cpp:1440 and :1464 (cmd_lightsleep), :1488 (cmd_deepsleep), components/hardwareone/System_BuildConfig.h:903-904 and :954 (NEOPIXEL_POWER_PIN 39 == I2C2_POWER_PIN 39), components/hardwareone/System_NeoPixel.cpp:162-166 (setLEDColor is the only pixels.show() path)
- **What:** oledPrepareForSleep() drives I2C2_POWER_PIN LOW whenever gSettings.oledBus == 1, which is the FeatherS3 default (OLED_BUS_DEFAULT resolves to 1 at System_BuildConfig.h:1088-1094). That pin is GPIO39, which components/arduino/variants/um_feathers3/pins_arduino.h defines as both RGB_PWR and LDO2 — so the on-board WS2812 loses Vcc along with the OLED. oledResumeFromSleep() raises GPIO39, waits 100 ms, runs I2C bus recovery and re-runs the SSD1306 begin()/rotation/brightness sequence, but never touches the NeoPixel.
- **Why it matters:** User sets a static LED color (`led red`), then runs `lightsleep 30`. GPIO39 goes LOW, the WS2812 loses power and its internal latch. On wake the OLED is fully restored but the LED stays off: setLEDColor() is only called on demand or from ledEffectTick() while an effect is active, so with no running effect nothing re-pushes the pixel. The LED silently disagrees with the firmware's own cached state until the next `led`/effect command.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED — board: FeatherS3 / FeatherS3[D] only (the only board with I2C2_POWER_PIN >= 0)
- **Direction:** Have oledResumeFromSleep (or a shared ldo2Restore() helper owned by neither subsystem) re-push the cached NeoPixel color after raising GPIO39, so both consumers of the shared rail recover symmetrically.

### [NOTE] 1 KB PSRAM memcpy runs inside taskENTER_CRITICAL on every OLED map frame, and the comment claims it is ~1 µs
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/OLED_Mode_Map.cpp:1199`
- **Also:** components/hardwareone/G2_Ring.cpp:145-155, :172, :184 (comparable shift loops, but internal DRAM only -- ~2 KB of struct moves, a few us)
- **What:** `if (sFrontBufReady) { taskENTER_CRITICAL(&sSwapSpinlock); memcpy(oledDisplay->getBuffer(), sMapFrontBuf, SSD1306_BUF_SIZE); taskEXIT_CRITICAL(&sSwapSpinlock); }` — SSD1306_BUF_SIZE is 1024 (:65) and `sMapFrontBuf` points at `sMapBufA`/`sMapBufB`, both `ps_malloc`'d into PSRAM (:929-930). The lock is genuinely required by the design (mapRenderTask can swap the pointers mid-copy at :1019-1023), so this is a note about cost and about the comment, not a correctness claim. The preceding comment reads 'Always blit front buffer to display (instant memcpy ~1us)'.
- **Why it matters:** I am reporting this only because my track owns P14 and because I can now close the question the prior write-up left open. It is NOT a P2 fault. `esp_ipc_isr_stall_other_cpu()` -- which `spi_flash_disable_interrupts_caches_and_other_cpu()` uses before turning the cache off -- fires on `ETS_IPC_ISR_INUM = 28` (esp-idf/components/soc/esp32s3/include/soc/soc.h:228), and `XCHAL_INT28_LEVEL = 4` (esp-idf/components/xtensa/esp32s3/include/xtensa/config/core-isa.h:411), above the level-3 mask `portENTER_CRITICAL` applies. So a core inside a critical section is still stalled at an instruction boundary before the cache goes away, and no PSRAM access under any first-party spinlock can fault. The residual cost is ~30 us of added interrupt latency per map frame on the rendering core.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED — independently reported by 2 finders; board: builds with ENABLE_MAPS + OLED — FeatherS3 today; prior art: The gRingTxQMux length is called out in the shared recon map (recon_isr.md §7) as a recon note; it has not been filed anywhere in docs/.
- **Direction:** The correctness fix is nil; the comment is simply wrong and should not be trusted as licence to add work here. If the cost ever matters, snapshotting the front-buffer pointer under the lock and copying outside it needs a way to stop the renderer reclaiming that buffer mid-copy.

### [NOTE] Comment claims the mapRender task has an "8KB stack in PSRAM" — xTaskCreatePinnedToCore cannot do that, and the project rule forbids it
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/OLED_Mode_Map.cpp:945`
- **What:** The comment above the task creation reads 'Create render task (8KB stack in PSRAM, low priority)'. The call on :949 is xTaskCreatePinnedToCore(..., MAP_RENDER_STACK_WORDS, ...), a dynamic create. IDF routes every dynamic FreeRTOS allocation through pvPortMalloc with portFREERTOS_HEAP_CAPS = MALLOC_CAP_INTERNAL|MALLOC_CAP_8BIT (esp-idf/components/freertos/heap_idf.c:43), so this stack is 8192 bytes of internal DRAM, not PSRAM. MAP_RENDER_STACK_WORDS is 8192 (System_TaskUtils.h:116) and StackType_t is uint8_t on this port, so the size itself is right.
- **Why it matters:** No runtime effect - the comment is simply false. It matters because it sits one file away from a real PSRAM task stack (finding #2), it contradicts the project rule recorded in docs/STACK_TO_PSRAM_CANDIDATES.md:200-206, and someone auditing 'which stacks are in PSRAM' by grepping comments would get two wrong answers in opposite directions. The PSRAM in this function is the double-buffer trio sMapBufA/B/sMapShadeBuf (:929-931), which is correct and correctly null-checked.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED — independently reported by 2 finders
- **Direction:** Reword to '8 KB internal-DRAM stack (xTaskCreate always allocates internal); the double buffers below are the PSRAM part'.


### Storage / filesystem — 6 findings (2 critical/high)

### [HIGH] appendLineWithCap rotation copies up to ~578 KB synchronously while holding the global FS mutex, on whichever task ran the command
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/System_Filesystem.cpp:1812`
- **Also:** Caps: System_Logging.h:54-58 (LOG_CAP_BYTES 696969, LOG_ERROR_CAP/LOG_EVENTS_CAP/LOG_EVENT_STREAM_CAP 256 KB, LOG_I2C_CAP 64 KB) and System_Utils.cpp:939 (command-audit.log 500 KB). Multi-log amplifiers: System_Debug.cpp:2333-2341 (logTimeSyncedMarkerIfReady writes SIX capped logs back to back) and :2398-2402 (logBootAnchorToLogs, four logs).
- **What:** `appendLineWithCap` takes `FsLockGuard guard("appendLineWithCap")` (:1817) for its whole body. Once the file exceeds its cap it streams the surviving 85 % into a `.tmp` in 1 KB chunks (:1887-1893), then `VFS::remove` + `VFS::rename` (:1901-1908). Caps in use: `command-audit.log` 500 KB (`System_Utils.cpp:939`, written from `logCommandExecution` for EVERY non-quiet command), `successful_login.log` / `failed_login.log` ~680 KB (`System_Debug.cpp:2333/2334/2399/2400`), `errors.log` / `system-events.log` / `events.log` 256 KB each. So one rotation copies 425 KB (audit) or ~578 KB (login logs) — read + write. `FsLockGuard` takes `gFsMutex` with `portMAX_DELAY` and no timeout (`System_Mutex.cpp:83-94`). `logCommandExecution` is called from `executeCommand` (`System_Utils.cpp:4744/4803/4815`), which runs directly on the httpd task (`WebServer_Server.cpp:2997`, `WebPage_ESPNow.cpp:563`, `WebPage_Bluetooth.cpp:41`) as well as on cmd_exec (`HardwareOne.cpp:743`).
- **Why it matters:** esp_littlefs configures block_size = 4096 (esp_littlefs.c:42/994) and cache_size = 512, so a 217 KB copy is ~53 fresh-block sector erases plus ~434 512-byte page-program bursts. Each of those is a separate spi_flash_disable_interrupts_caches_and_other_cpu() window: the initiating core suspends its scheduler and masks non-IRAM interrupts, and the OTHER core is IPC-parked spinning in spi_flash_op_block_func with its scheduler suspended too (cache_utils.c:89-192). A 4 KB sector erase holds that window for the entire erase — spi_flash_chip_generic_erase_sector busy-polls wait_idle with esp_rom_delay_us and never re-enables the cache mid-sector (spi_flash_chip_generic.c:212-236, 424-478); CONFIG_SPI_FLASH_ERASE_YIELD_DURATION_MS=20 only yields BETWEEN sectors (esp_flash_api.c:662-668). At ~25-50 ms typical per sector erase (IDF's own timeout for this build is 600 ms, spi_flash_chip_generic.c:66) that is roughly 2.0-2.5 s of near-total dual-core unavailability for a 256 KB log, ~4-5 s for command-audit.log, ~6 s for a login log. Most of these rotations execute on debug_out (System_Debug.cpp:308/322/332, core 0, the single writer for Serial + web mirror + BLE output), so all log output freezes for the duration and gDebugDropped starts counting once the 64-192 slot queue fills. Everything else that touches the FS blocks on gFsMutex for the same window — including isAdminUser(), which takes FsLockGuard on EVERY command authorization (System_User.cpp:298), so command dispatch across CLI/web/BLE/ESP-NOW stalls too. On the radio side the WiFi task, espnow_task and the Bluedroid BTC/BTU tasks are all pinned to core 0 and are unschedulable through each window; the 8-slot ESP-NOW RX ring (System_ESPNow.cpp:393) plus 16 static WiFi RX buffers absorb roughly 0.4 s at the measured ~37 frames/s, after which frames are dropped, and the 8-slot R1 ring TX queue (G2_Ring.cpp:111) overflows. Worst realistic case is logTimeSyncedMarkerIfReady() at first NTP sync, which can chain up to six rotations (~1.86 MB of copying, ~20 s) at exactly the moment WiFi has just come up.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED — independently reported by 2 finders; prior art: docs/HEAP_OFFLOAD_SWEEP_2026-07-24.md §2b touches "snprintf'd after any LittleFS op returns"; recon_persist.md item 1 covers the *orphan-recovery* gap in this same function. The stall/lock-hold cost i
- **Direction:** Rotation should not be synchronous inside the logging path. Options in order of cost: bound the work per call (copy a slice per invocation and finish across several calls), do the swap by writing forward into a second file and switching the active name instead of copying the tail, or move rotation to a low-priority worker that does not hold gFsMutex across the whole copy. Also consider shrinking the login-log caps — 696969 B is the single largest rotation in the tree.

### [HIGH] readTextLimited() reserves the FULL byte cap before reading, ignores the reserve failure, and degrades to a per-16-byte realloc treadmill exactly when DRAM is tight
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/System_Filesystem.cpp:1767`
- **Also:** Callers: components/hardwareone/G2_Page_Files.cpp:627 (cap 12288, internal) and components/hardwareone/System_Filesystem.cpp:736 (cap 60000, PSRAM). Note docs/HOT_PATH_HEAP_AUDIT_MECHANICS.md:209 explicitly holds this function up as "the correct shape already in-tree" and prescribes copying it into readText() — doing so would import the eager full-cap reserve into the 4 Hz automation tick.
- **What:** readTextLimited does `out.reserve(maxBytes);` unconditionally — before it knows the file size — then fills `out` with `for (int i=0;i<n;++i) out += buf[i];`. The reserve() return value is discarded. With CONFIG_SPIRAM_MALLOC_ALWAYSINTERNAL=16384, heap_caps_malloc_default routes `size <= 16384` to MALLOC_CAP_INTERNAL (verified in esp-idf/components/heap/heap_caps.c:117-121), so a cap at or below 16 KB is an *internal DRAM* allocation. G2_Page_Files.cpp:627 passes kFilesTextReadCapBytes = 12*1024, so String::changeBuffer requests (12288+16)&~0xf = 12,304 B → served from internal DRAM.
- **Why it matters:** Opening ANY text/CSV/JSON file in the G2 Files viewer — even a 40-byte one — demands a single contiguous 12,304-byte internal-DRAM block, on the g2_tap_disp worker, at precisely the moment the heap is most fragmented: dual NimBLE GATT clients up, g2_session_w's 8 KB stack claimed. The developer's own comments in G2_Glasses.cpp record largest-free-block ≈9 KB under exactly those conditions. When reserve() fails, nothing notices: the loop proceeds with single-char String::operator+=, each of which re-enters changeBuffer with 16-byte-granular exact-fit growth → ~768 realloc+memcpy steps for a 12 KB file, walking a growing block across the internal heap and leaving a trail of 16-byte-larger holes. That is the textbook P7 fragmentation pathology, and it only fires under memory pressure — i.e. it makes a tight heap worse, never better. The fileview CLI path (System_Filesystem.cpp:736, cap 60000) escapes the eager-internal problem (60016 > 16384 → PSRAM) but hits the same silent-degradation path if PSRAM is exhausted, at ~3,750 reallocs.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED — board: all; the 12 KB internal variant needs ENABLE_G2_GLASSES=1 (on for the current FeatherS3 config); prior art: docs/HOT_PATH_HEAP_AUDIT_MECHANICS.md:209 cites this function as the fix template — the prior doc was wrong about it.
- **Direction:** Reserve min(maxBytes, f.size()) rather than maxBytes, check the reserve result and fail closed if it returns false, and replace the per-char append with a single out.concat(buf, n) per chunk so the loop cannot silently become a realloc treadmill.

### [MEDIUM] The boot corrupt-JSON check still guards the dead root path /settings.json, so the live /system/settings.json has no integrity check at all - and the debug.json split edited that same list without fixing it
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/System_Filesystem.cpp:175`
- **What:** The boot JSON validation loop checks `{"/settings.json", "/system/debug.json", "/system/automations.json", "/system/users/users.json"}`. SETTINGS_JSON_FILE is `/system/settings.json` (HardwareOne.cpp:240), so the first entry never matches anything and the live settings file gets no check at all. The test itself is `content[0] != '{' && != '['` — a first-character heuristic, not a parse — and the action is `VFS::removeGuarded` (delete), not quarantine, which is the opposite of the users.json handling added later (quarantine to .bad, System_User.cpp:3468-3476).
- **Why it matters:** The check exists precisely to catch a corrupt settings file at boot and it is pointed at a path the firmware has not written since the IDF migration - so a corrupt /system/settings.json is never detected here. Two further problems in the same block: the test is a first-character heuristic (content[0] != '{' && != '[', :181) rather than a parse, and the action is VFS::removeGuarded - delete, not quarantine. For /system/users/users.json that delete pre-empts the far more careful quarantine-to-.bad path in loadAndIncrementBootSeq (System_User.cpp:3466-3472), which runs later at :194. The debug-file split added /system/debug.json to this array, which is direct evidence the list was recently edited and the stale settings entry was walked past.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED — independently reported by 3 finders; prior art: KNOWN - docs/SETTINGS_LIFECYCLE_AUDIT.md §4.3 (MED). Status unchanged. Re-reported only because the debug split touched this exact array and left the defect, and because the users.json delete now demo
- **Direction:** Point the entry at SETTINGS_JSON_FILE, replace the first-character heuristic with an actual deserializeJson attempt, and rename to <path>.bad instead of deleting - matching what the users.json path already does.

### [MEDIUM] ramflush treats WiFi link state as the live signal, so a transient AP outage at capture time suppresses the configured WiFi autostart on the next boot
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/System_RamFlush.cpp:206`
- **What:** ramFlushReadLive(RF_WIFI) returns WiFi.isConnected() (:206) while ramFlushReadIntent(RF_WIFI) returns gSettings.wifiAutoStart (:255). Every other feature's live getter reports a started/initialised state that the deliberate comments in this file were careful to pick (RF_BLUETOOTH uses gBLEState->initialized not isBLERunning(), :186-192; RF_MQTT uses isMqttStarted() not isMqttConnected(), :178-181; RF_OLED uses gOledRunning not oledConnected, :208-212; RF_AUTOMATION uses gAutomationSchedulerRunning not automationEnabled, :217-221). WiFi is the one feature keyed to an association rather than an intent. Nothing marks a WiFi drop as non-user-caused: sAutostartFailed is only set from System_I2C.cpp:3033 and System_Camera_DVP.cpp:947.
- **Why it matters:** WiFi came up fine at boot with wifiAutoStart=true; hours later the AP reboots and the link is momentarily down when the user runs `ramflush`. Capture sees isLive=false vs intent=true, records diverge with live=0 (:306-311). Next boot HardwareOne.cpp:1700 evaluates ramFlushResolve(RF_WIFI, wifiAutoStart) → the overlay bit wins → false → setupWiFi() is skipped entirely, and http and mqtt (both gated on WiFi being up) do not start either. The device comes back with networking off until someone issues `openwifi` manually. A reboot-and-resume command silently disabled the user's configured autostart.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED — board: all (ENABLE_WIFI); prior art: docs/RAMFLUSH_IMPLEMENTATION_MAP.md §1.4 chose WiFi.isConnected() as the live getter and §1.4's accepted-asymmetry note covers the reverse case (resume ON but AP out of range). This direction — resume
- **Direction:** Key the WiFi live read to a started/intended state (a WiFi-subsystem-running flag, or WiFi.getMode() plus a persisted 'user turned WiFi off' bit as WIFI_RADIO_OWNERSHIP_PLAN D5 proposes) rather than link association, or treat 'not connected but the stack is up' as non-divergent.

### [LOW] ramflush records WiFi and HTTP as "user turned it off" when they were merely disconnected or failed at capture time - the autostart-failed guard covers sensors and camera but not the network features
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/System_RamFlush.cpp:304`
- **What:** ramFlushCaptureOverlay skips a feature whose bit is set in sAutostartFailed (:304), so a feature that failed to autostart is not recorded as user intent. sAutostartFailed is only ever raised from System_I2C.cpp:3033 (via ramFlushIdForModule, which maps only thermal/tof/imu/gps/fmradio/apds/gamepad/rtc/presence - :382-391) and System_Camera_DVP.cpp:947 for RF_CAMERA. RF_WIFI's live read is WiFi.isConnected() (:206) and RF_HTTP's is isHttpServerRunning() (:165); neither has a failed-autostart path.
- **Why it matters:** Run `ramflush` while WiFi happens to be mid-reconnect or the configured AP is briefly unreachable: live=false, intent=wifiAutoStart=true -> divergence recorded with the live bit clear. The resumed boot then calls ramFlushResolve(RF_WIFI, wifiAutoStart) (HardwareOne.cpp:1743), gets false, and skips the WiFi connect entirely - on a headless device that means no network until another reboot. HTTP inherits it: with WiFi down isHttpServerRunning() is false, so RF_HTTP diverges too and the web server is skipped on the same boot. Self-heals on a normal reboot, which is why this is LOW rather than higher.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED
- **Direction:** Give RF_WIFI and RF_HTTP the same sAutostartFailed treatment the sensors get (raise the bit when the boot-time connect/start attempt fails), or exclude features whose live signal is a transient link state rather than a user-controlled on/off.

### [NOTE] initFilesystem() gates the automations.json boot sanitation on gSettings.automationEnabled, which is read before settings are loaded and is always the constructor default
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/System_Filesystem.cpp:207`
- **What:** `if (gSettings.automationEnabled && VFS::existsGuarded(AUTOMATIONS_JSON_FILE, ...))` with the comment 'Skip if automation system is disabled'. initFilesystem() is called at HardwareOne.cpp:1310, while settingsDefaults() (which runs applyRegisteredDefaults) is at :1336 and readSettingsJson() at :1345. So the value read here is always the struct's NSDMI, automationEnabled(true) at System_Settings.h:239.
- **Why it matters:** The gate never actually skips: a user who persisted automationsEnabled=false still pays the read + sanitize pass every boot. Harmless today because the ctor default and the registry default (System_Automation.cpp:4174, intDefault true) agree and the pass is read-mostly. It matters as a pattern — any future gSettings read added to initFilesystem() will silently use ctor defaults rather than persisted values, with no compiler or runtime signal.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED — board: all (ENABLE_AUTOMATION)
- **Direction:** Move the automations sanitation out of initFilesystem() to after readSettingsJson(), or drop the misleading gate and its comment.


### Automation / autostart — 5 findings (3 critical/high)

### [HIGH] Automation scheduler cache freed from cmd_exec_task while loopTask reads and writes it
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/System_Automation.cpp:535`
- **What:** `releaseAutomationsCache()` (:533-541) does `heap_caps_free(gAutomationsCache); gAutomationsCache = nullptr;` and only then sets `gAutomationsCacheCount = 0`. `gAutomationsCache` is a plain non-volatile pointer (:497) while the count and valid flag are volatile (:498-499). It is called from `stopAutomationScheduler()` (:4119-4123), which `automation system disable` reaches at :1998.
- **Why it matters:** `cmd_automation` runs on cmd_exec_task (core 0) and is reachable from serial, /api/cli, BLE, ESP-NOW remote exec, MQTT, OLED and G2. The two users of the cache both run on loopTask (core 1): `automationsAnyDue()` (:600-609) indexes `gAutomationsCache[i]` after a plain null-check, and `rebuildAutomationsCache()` (:545-595) *writes* `gAutomationsCache[n].id/.enabled/.nextAt` at :564-589 inside a loop over an ArduinoJson array. Both are entered from HardwareOne.cpp:2399/2404. schedulerTickMinute reads and parses automations.json before rebuilding, so the window is tens of milliseconds wide. A free landing inside rebuild is either a store through nullptr (StoreProhibited panic) or a write into a freed, unpoisoned, immediately reusable heap block — heap corruption in another owner's allocation. The read path is milder but still wrong: because the pointer is retired before the count, automationsAnyDue can index a freed slot, and garbage there makes it return true, which calls schedulerTickMinute, which re-allocates and re-runs the scheduler the user just disabled.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED — board: all builds with ENABLE_AUTOMATION — System_BuildConfig.h:315 sets it to 1, so this applies to FeatherS3
- **Direction:** This file already contains the correct decision for the sibling buffer and the reasoning behind it. System_Automation.cpp:3691-3695 says of sEventBuf: "Deliberately never freed. cmd_automation flips automationEnabled from the command tasks (cmd_exec/web, core 0) while a tick may be mid-scan on the loop task (core 1), so releasing it on disable would hand a live reader a dangling pointer." The scheduler cache does exactly what that comment forbids; the cheapest fix is to make it match (allocate once, keep, just invalidate).

### [HIGH] automationsAnyDue() walks gAutomationsCache on core 1 after stopAutomationScheduler() freed it on core 0 — pointer retired before the count
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/System_Automation.cpp:533`
- **Also:** reader gate at components/hardwareone/HardwareOne.cpp:2443-2457; writer entry point at components/hardwareone/System_Automation.cpp:4119
- **What:** releaseAutomationsCache() does `heap_caps_free(gAutomationsCache); gAutomationsCache = nullptr;` FIRST, then `gAutomationsCacheCount = 0; gAutomationsCacheValid = false;` (:533-541). automationsAnyDue() (:600-609) tests `if (!gAutomationsCache) return true;` and `if (!gAutomationsCacheValid) return true;` once on entry, then iterates `for (int i = 0; i < gAutomationsCacheCount; i++) gAutomationsCache[i].enabled ...`. gAutomationsCacheCount and gAutomationsCacheValid are volatile; gAutomationsCache itself is a plain non-volatile pointer. The loop body contains no function calls, so at -O2 the compiler is free to keep the base pointer in a register across all 64 iterations.
- **Why it matters:** automationsAnyDue() runs on loopTask, core 1 (CONFIG_ARDUINO_RUNNING_CORE=1), from HardwareOne.cpp:2455. releaseAutomationsCache() is reached from `automation system disable` (System_Automation.cpp:1996-1998) on cmd_exec_task, core 0, and from suspendAutomationSystem() (:957). The loop's outer gate at HardwareOne.cpp:2443 reads gSettings.automationEnabled and gAutomationSchedulerRunning — both plain non-volatile bools — so core 1 can pass that gate, get preempted, watch core 0 clear the flags and free the 832-byte internal-DRAM block, then resume and read it. This is the identical store-order defect A8 filed against GPSTrackManager::clearTrack(): the count must retire before the pointer, and here it retires after. Outcome is a read of freed internal DRAM (garbage enabled/nextAt → one spurious schedulerTickMinute, which self-heals via ensureAutomationsCache), or, if the compiler reloads the global inside the loop, a null dereference and a LoadProhibited panic.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED — board: all builds with ENABLE_AUTOMATION (FeatherS3 included)
- **Direction:** Store order is backwards for any lock-free reader — null the count and the valid flag before freeing the block, and make gAutomationsCache itself volatile so the reader cannot cache a stale base. Longer term the disable path should quiesce the loop-side reader rather than free under it; the cache is only 832 bytes, so simply never freeing it is also a defensible answer here.

### [HIGH] executeConditionalCommand() scans unbounded past the end of the command string when the command is exactly "IF " or "IF x" (size_t underflow in the loop bound)
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/System_Automation.cpp:3376`
- **Also:** cmd_conditional (HardwareOne.cpp:1226) calls the same function with raw CLI args, but it is not registered in the `commands[]` table (System_Utils.cpp:2619), so today the only live entry is the automation runner.
- **What:** `size_t cmdLen = strlen(command);` then, after confirming the first three chars are "IF ", `for (size_t i = 3; i < cmdLen - 5; i++) { ... cmdStr[i+1] ... cmdStr[i+5] ... }`. For cmdLen == 3 or 4, `cmdLen - 5` underflows to ~4.29e9 and the loop reads forward from the end of the command buffer looking for a case-insensitive " THEN " until it finds one or faults. The same pattern is repeated at :3396 for the ELSE scan.
- **Why it matters:** An automation whose command list contains the literal `IF ` (3 chars) or `IF x` (4 chars) — reachable from `automationadd`, from an edited automations.json, and from the boot/scheduled/manual run paths (System_Automation.cpp:894, :1838, :4030 all feed cmdsList entries straight into executeConditionalCommand). The scan walks off the heap allocation into unmapped memory and takes a LoadProhibited panic, or it "finds" a phantom THEN at a huge offset and the follow-on `strncpy(thenBuf, cmdStr + thenStart, 127)` at :3426 reads from a wild address. Note cmdLen 5, 6 and up are all safe — only 3 and 4 underflow, which is exactly why this survives normal use.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED — board: boards with ENABLE_AUTOMATION (System_BuildConfig.h:315 — unconditional 1)
- **Direction:** Guard the scans with `if (cmdLen >= 6)` (or iterate `i + 5 < cmdLen`) before either THEN/ELSE search. The sibling parser in evaluateCondition (:2578) already uses a NUL-terminated pointer walk that short-circuits correctly — that form is the safer template.

### [MEDIUM] An interval automation trigger below 1000 ms rewrites all of automations.json on every main-loop pass, unbounded
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/System_Automation.cpp:2318`
- **Also:** recomputeAllNextAt (:2084) writes once per automation rather than once per pass.
- **What:** nextFire() for Trigger::INTERVAL returns from + (time_t)(s.intervalMs / 1000). The add-trigger validator (:1111-1115) only requires isNumeric(intervalMs) — there is no minimum. With intervalMs < 1000 the integer division yields 0, so rescheduleAfterFire (:2434-2473) stores nextAt == firedAt. automationsAnyDue uses now >= nextAt (:604), so the trigger is due again immediately; HardwareOne.cpp:2398-2405 then sets needFullTick on every loop iteration. Each fire performs readText of the whole file, deserializeJson, serializeJsonPretty and writeAutomationsJsonAtomic (:2469-2472), which also invalidates the cache and re-forces a full tick.
- **Why it matters:** One admin-created automation with intervalms=500 puts the device into a permanent loop of full-file read + parse + pretty-serialize + tmp write + rename at main-loop rate. On a flash-backed LittleFS that is continuous erase/program traffic against the same blocks, plus a cache-disabling flash operation on every pass — a wear and latency problem with no throttle and no diagnostic. Even a legal intervalms=1000 costs one full rewrite of automations.json per second (~86k/day).
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED
- **Direction:** Enforce a minimum intervalMs (>= 1000, or better, compute nextFire in milliseconds) in the trigger validator, and add a floor in nextFire so a stored sub-second value can never yield nextAt == firedAt. A dirty-flag debounce before writeAutomationsJsonAtomic would also cap the write rate independently.

### [MEDIUM] `time(nullptr) > 0` is used as an "is the clock synced?" test, but pre-sync `time()` returns uptime seconds — so the test is always true after 1 s of uptime
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/System_Automation.cpp:3701`
- **Also:** Same broken predicate: System_Automation.cpp:921 and :1855; System_Utils.cpp:751 (`timeSyncUpdateBootEpoch`); WebServer_Server.cpp:686 (`buildAllSessionsJson` -> admin session view renders 1970 dates when unsynced); HardwareOne.cpp:1451 (`resolvePendingUserCreationTimes` runs a guaranteed-failing pass — System_User.cpp:3100 rejects every record because `createdAtUtc > now + 60`); System_ESPNow_Identity.cpp:221/485; System_ESPNow_Handlers_Crypto.cpp:292/397. Correct sites for contrast: System_ESPNow.cpp:10000, HardwareOne.cpp:2105, System_Maps.cpp:2946, System_ImageManager.cpp:138, System_Debug.cpp:1678 all use an epoch-threshold compare.
- **What:** `schedulerTickMinute()` computes `bool timeValid = (now > 0);` and line 3762 comments "Without synced time only event fires are possible; skip the clock scan" — but the gate never trips. Under `CONFIG_LIBC_TIME_SYSCALL_USE_RTC_HRT` (set in sdkconfig:2477), ESP-IDF's `_gettimeofday_r` returns `get_adjusted_boot_time() + esp_time_impl_get_time_since_boot()` (esp-idf/components/newlib/src/time.c:173). Before any `settimeofday`, the boot-time base is 0, so `time(nullptr)` returns *seconds since boot*. So `now > 0` is true from ~1 s of uptime onward and `timeValid` is effectively a constant `true`. The clock scan at 3838-3839 then runs `computeNextRunTime(obj, now)` and `now >= nextAt` against a 1970-era `now`. This file already includes System_Clock.h and uses `Clock::isSynced()` at :2914 — the correct predicate was available and not used.
- **Why it matters:** (a) Device boots with no network. `automation add` -> `computeInitialNextAt` (:1305-1322) stamps `nextAt` from the 1970 clock, e.g. an interval trigger gets `nextAt = 40 + 3600`. Persisted to automations.json. WiFi later comes up, NTP sets the clock to ~1.79e9 -> every such trigger satisfies `now >= nextAt` -> ALL of them fire at once, then reschedule correctly. (b) Permanently-offline device: `nextFire()` TIME triggers run `localtime_r`/`mktime` on 1970 dates, so "daily at 07:00" resolves to 1970-01-01 07:00 = 25200 and fires at 7 hours of *uptime*, then every 24 h of uptime. The scheduler silently degrades from a wall clock to an uptime timer instead of taking the documented event-only path.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED
- **Direction:** Replace every `time(nullptr) > 0` sync test with `Clock::isSynced()` (or `Clock::isValidEpoch(t)`), which is exactly the helper System_Clock.h was created to centralize. `now > 0` distinguishes nothing on this platform.


### MQTT — 5 findings (1 critical/high)

### [HIGH] MQTT topic/payload are treated as NUL-terminated C strings — unbounded strlen over the esp-mqtt receive buffer
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/System_MQTT.cpp:149`
- **What:** `updateExternalSensor` builds `String topicStr = String(topic).substring(0, topicLen);` and `String dataStr = String(data).substring(0, dataLen);` (:149-150). `String(const char*)` calls `strlen()` on the pointer before any truncation, so the length arguments the function was given are applied only after the read has already happened.
- **Why it matters:** esp-mqtt's `topic`/`data` are NOT NUL-terminated: `deliver_publish` sets `client->event.topic = msg_topic` and `client->event.data = msg_data` as raw pointers into `client->mqtt_state.in_buffer` with separate `topic_len`/`data_len` (esp-idf/components/mqtt/esp-mqtt/mqtt_client.c:1124-1128). Topic and payload are adjacent in that buffer with no terminator between them, and the residue after the current packet is whatever the previous packet left. The same file proves the author knows this — the sibling log line at :953-955 correctly uses `%.*s` with explicit lengths, and `handleMQTTCommand` at :426-436 correctly uses `strncmp(topic, ..., topicLen)` and a byte loop. Trigger: with `mqttSubscribeExternal` on and a matching subscription, a publisher (or the broker) first sends a large message whose topic+payload contain no 0x00 byte, filling `in_buffer` (default 1024 B) with non-zero bytes, then sends a short message on the same topic. `String(topic)` then walks the entire 1 KB buffer and past the end of the heap allocation until it finds a zero byte — an out-of-bounds heap read on the esp-mqtt task, and an attacker-sized String allocation before `.substring()` throws it away. On ESP32-S3 walking off the end of a heap region raises LoadProhibited.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED — board: all (ENABLE_MQTT)
- **Direction:** Construct both with the explicit two-argument form (`String(topic, topicLen)`) or copy through a length-bounded buffer, exactly as `handleMQTTCommand` already does at :426-436. Nothing else in the function needs to change.

### [MEDIUM] MQTT start discards `esp_mqtt_client_start()` and `esp_mqtt_client_register_event()`, then unconditionally sets `mqttClientRunning = true` and returns success
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/System_MQTT.cpp:1074`
- **Also:** All 15 `esp_mqtt_client_publish` sites discard the msg_id (-1 = failure), including the command-response publishes at :448/:460/:477/:497/:504/:511
- **What:** esp_mqtt_client_init's null return is checked, but the two calls that actually bring the client up — esp_mqtt_client_register_event(mqttClient, MQTT_EVENT_ANY, mqtt_event_handler, nullptr) at System_MQTT.cpp:1073 and esp_mqtt_client_start(mqttClient) at :1074 — have their esp_err_t discarded. The code then sets mqttClientRunning = true, clears lastError, broadcasts "[MQTT] Client started" and returns true.
- **Why it matters:** `esp_mqtt_client_start` returns ESP_FAIL when its internal `xTaskCreate` for `mqtt_task` fails — a 6 KB internal-DRAM stack, on a device the prior heap work measures at 29–39 KB free internal DRAM with the LLM loaded (docs/HOT_PATH_HEAP_AUDIT_MECHANICS.md §1). When it does: the command returns success, `mqttstatus` reports running, `lastError` is cleared to empty, and no `SYSEVT_MQTT_START_FAILED` is posted — but the socket never opens, no `MQTT_EVENT_CONNECTED` ever arrives, `mqttTofConnected` stays false, and every subsequent `esp_mqtt_client_publish` (15 sites, all also unchecked) is a no-op. Home-Assistant discovery, availability LWT and the whole sensor bridge are silently dead with the UI showing green. Operationally this is the worst kind of failure for a bridge: it is indistinguishable from "broker is up but quiet".
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED — independently reported by 2 finders
- **Direction:** Check both returns; on failure destroy the client, restore `lastError`, post `SYSEVT_MQTT_START_FAILED` and return false so the existing failure plumbing (which is already correct for the two earlier steps) actually fires.

### [MEDIUM] MQTT Home Assistant discovery builder writes past a 1024-byte PSRAM buffer when `mqttBaseTopic` is long; SETTING_STRING has no length validation anywhere
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/System_MQTT.cpp:378`
- **Also:** System_MQTT.cpp:703-739 `publishPeerDiscoveryConfig` has the same chain into the same shared buffer, and additionally interpolates remote-peer-supplied `peer.friendlyName[48]` and `peer.room[32]` from ESP-NOW metadata.
- **What:** `publishDiscoveryConfig` chains seven `pos += snprintf(configJson + pos, sizeof(configJson) - pos, ...)` calls into `sMqttDiscoveryConfigJson[1024]` (a real 1024-byte EXT_RAM_BSS array, not an over-sized one) with no clamp between them. `pos` is `int`, `sizeof(configJson)` is `size_t` — once `pos` > 1024 the size argument wraps to ~4.29e9 and every subsequent call writes unbounded at `configJson + pos`. `gSettings.mqttBaseTopic` is interpolated twice (as `stateTopic` and `availTopic`), and the generic SETTING_STRING apply path is `*((String*)entry->valuePtr) = p;` with no length check at all (System_Settings.cpp:2676), while the registry entry itself passes 0/0 for min/max (System_MQTT.cpp:268).
- **Why it matters:** Set `mqttbasetopic` to ~600 characters (legal today — nothing validates it, and the CLI allows 2047-byte lines) and enable MQTT discovery. On connect, the first snprintf consumes most of the 1024 bytes, the availability-topic snprintf pushes `pos` to ~1500, and the `",\"device\":{...}"` snprintf at :378 then writes ~90 bytes at offset 1500 — roughly 570 bytes past the end of the PSRAM BSS array, into whatever follows it. `publishDiscoveryConfig` runs ~40 times back-to-back per connect, so the corruption repeats.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED
- **Direction:** Clamp `pos` to `sizeof(configJson)-1` after each append and bail out of the builder when the JSON does not fit (a truncated HA discovery payload is a bug on its own). Separately, SETTING_STRING needs a length bound in the apply path — right now no string setting anywhere in the firmware is length-checked.

### [MEDIUM] MQTT mesh-routing branch runs command handlers on the esp-mqtt event task, using the same shared static buffers the local path was moved off that task to avoid
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/System_MQTT.cpp:492`
- **Also:** Shared buffers written by the called handlers: getDebugBuffer() in cmd_espnow_roomcmd (System_ESPNow.cpp:11854) and `EXT_RAM_BSS_ATTR static char remoteBuffer[256]` in cmd_espnow_remote (:14356).
- **What:** `handleMQTTCommand` is invoked directly from the `MQTT_EVENT_DATA` case of the esp-mqtt event handler (System_MQTT.cpp:947), i.e. on the esp-mqtt client task, whose stack is the IDF default (no `mqtt_cfg.task_stack` override anywhere in the file). The mesh branch calls `cmd_espnow_roomcmd`/`cmd_espnow_remote` inline on that task. Those handlers write their results into process-global statics and perform synchronous AEAD sends (`espnowtx::sendAeadSync` with a 2000 ms timeout). The comment at :520-524 explains the local path was deliberately moved to cmd_exec_task precisely to get "no concurrent-handler races on shared state / static response buffers" and an 8 KB stack — and then states the mesh path was left as-is.
- **Why it matters:** A concurrent CLI/web/ESP-NOW invocation of `espnowremote` running on cmd_exec_task and an MQTT `target:device:` command running on the esp-mqtt task both write `remoteBuffer[256]` / the global debug buffer with no lock; the result string returned to one caller can be the other's. `cmd_espnow_roomcmd` additionally blocks the MQTT event task for up to 2 s per matched peer inside `sendAeadSync`, stalling the broker connection's event pump for the whole fan-out.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED — board: All boards with ENABLE_MQTT=1; not compiled in the current default config.
- **Direction:** Route the mesh branch through `submitAndExecuteSync` like the local branch — that fixes the authorization bypass, the task-context problem and the shared-buffer race in one change.

### [LOW] `mqttstatus` "Next Publish" underflows to ~4.29e9 ms
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/System_MQTT.cpp:1336`
- **What:** `unsigned long nextPublish = (lastPublishTime + gSettings.mqttPublishIntervalMs) - millis();` with no clamp. `lastPublishTime` is only advanced inside `mqttTick()` (:1264), which requires `mqttClientRunning && mqttClient && mqttTofConnected`.
- **Why it matters:** Whenever millis() has already passed lastPublishTime + interval — which is the normal state any time a publish is due or the MQTT loop has stalled — the unsigned subtraction wraps and the very next line reports e.g. 'Next Publish: 4294962184 ms'. Cosmetic only (the value is printed, never used for scheduling), but it makes `mqttstatus` actively misleading during exactly the situation an operator would run it.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED — independently reported by 2 finders
- **Direction:** Compute elapsed first and clamp: `unsigned long elapsed = millis() - lastPublishTime; unsigned long next = (elapsed >= interval) ? 0 : (interval - elapsed);` and special-case `lastPublishTime == 0` as "pending first publish".


### Settings / config — 21 findings (0 critical/high)

### [MEDIUM] The base-XIAO board block is unreachable dead code — XIAO_ESP32S3_SENSE_ENABLED is defined unconditionally
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/System_BuildConfig.h:176`
- **Also:** components/hardwareone/System_BuildConfig.h:813 (Sense #elif that always wins), :854-875 (base-XIAO #elif, unreachable), :184 (ENABLE_CAMERA_SENSOR), :195 (ENABLE_MICROPHONE_SENSOR)
- **What:** `#define XIAO_ESP32S3_SENSE_ENABLED 1` sits at file scope, unguarded, before the board chain. The Sense branch is `#elif defined(ARDUINO_XIAO_ESP32S3_SENSE_DEV) || (defined(ARDUINO_XIAO_ESP32S3_DEV) && defined(XIAO_ESP32S3_SENSE_ENABLED))`, so whenever ARDUINO_XIAO_ESP32S3_DEV is set the Sense branch matches and the following `#elif defined(ARDUINO_XIAO_ESP32S3_DEV)` base-board block can never compile. The comment at :173-175 claims the define "goes inert on the FeatherS3" — true for FeatherS3, false for the base XIAO, which is the board it was actually meant to distinguish.
- **Why it matters:** Building HW_BOARD=xiao_s3 for a bare XIAO ESP32-S3 (no expansion board) silently produces a Sense image: ENABLE_CAMERA_SENSOR=1 so initCamera drives GPIO10/11/12/13/14/15/16/17/18/38/39/40/47/48; ENABLE_MICROPHONE_SENSOR=1 so I2S PDM claims GPIO42/41; SD_CS/SCK/MISO/MOSI claim GPIO21/7/8/9 and SPI is brought up against nothing; and USER_LED_PIN is forced to -1 so the board's actual LED on GPIO21 is unusable. The intended base-board pin map (USER_LED_PIN 21, no camera/mic/SD) can never be selected.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED — board: Seeed XIAO ESP32-S3 (base, non-Sense)
- **Direction:** Move the define under a guard the board file can set (e.g. only define it when the Sense expansion is actually present, or key it off a separate HW_BOARD entry such as boards/xiao_s3_sense.defaults) so the base and Sense pin maps are both reachable.

### [MEDIUM] XIAO_ESP32S3_Plus is documented as a supported variant but has no board block; it falls into the unsupported #else with SDA=21/SCL=22, and GPIO22 does not exist on ESP32-S3
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/System_BuildConfig.h:984`
- **Also:** components/hardwareone/System_BuildConfig.h:978-993 (unsupported fallback), :1100-1104 (REQUIRE_SUPPORTED_BOARD commented out, so only a #warning); docs/BOARD_SWITCHING.md:151-163, :246, :336, :341
- **What:** There is no `#elif defined(ARDUINO_XIAO_ESP32S3_PLUS_DEV)` in the board chain, so CONFIG_ARDUINO_VARIANT="XIAO_ESP32S3_Plus" lands in the final #else: BOARD_SUPPORTED 0, I2C_SDA_PIN_DEFAULT 21, I2C_SCL_PIN_DEFAULT 22, no NeoPixel, no battery. REQUIRE_SUPPORTED_BOARD is commented out, so this is a warning, not an error. docs/BOARD_SWITCHING.md lists the Plus with its own settings table, names XIAO_ESP32S3_Plus among "the valid values" for CONFIG_ARDUINO_VARIANT (:341), and at :336 tells the user to reach it by editing boards/xiao_s3.defaults — the exact path into this fallback.
- **Why it matters:** GPIO22 is not a real pin on ESP32-S3: SOC_GPIO_VALID_GPIO_MASK (esp-idf soc/esp32s3/include/soc/soc_caps.h:187) masks out BIT22-BIT25, so i2c_master.c:966's GPIO_IS_VALID_GPIO check rejects it and bus 0 never initializes. The user sees "no I2C devices found" plus a buried #warning rather than "this board is not supported". GPIO21 is also wrong — on the XIAO family it is the user LED / SD CS.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED — board: Seeed XIAO ESP32-S3 Plus
- **Direction:** Either add a real XIAO Plus block (with boards/xiao_s3_plus.defaults at 16 MB flash) or remove the Plus from docs/BOARD_SWITCHING.md. Turning on REQUIRE_SUPPORTED_BOARD, or at minimum making the #else fallback a #error for ESP32-S3 targets, would convert this whole class into a build failure.

### [MEDIUM] KNOWN, unchanged — gDeferWrites is a single global with no timeout and exactly one clear site, so an abandoned web batch silently turns every setting change on every surface into RAM-only until reboot
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/System_Settings.cpp:3064`
- **What:** cmd_beginwrite sets gDeferWrites = true (:3064). The only clear is cmd_savesettings (:3070). The web settings page issues ['beginwrite', ...cmds, 'savesettings'] through /api/cli/batch (WebPage_Settings.h:113). While the flag is set, setSetting (System_Settings.h:1150/1159/1168), setDebugSetting (:1181) and all four handleSettingCommand branches (:2645, :2660, :2668, :2677) skip their write, and handleSettingCommand still returns '[Settings] Configuration updated'.
- **Why it matters:** If the batch aborts between beginwrite and savesettings (client navigates away, socket drops, an intermediate command errors and the page's sendSequential stops), the flag is stranded true for the rest of the boot. Because the flag is global rather than per-session, changes made concurrently from serial, OLED, G2, MQTT or an automation during that window are also silently discarded — each one reporting success. Re-verified at HEAD: no watchdog, timeout, or endpoint-completion clear exists.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED — prior art: docs/SETTINGS_LIFECYCLE_AUDIT.md §4.6
- **Direction:** Clear gDeferWrites unconditionally when the /api/cli/batch handler returns (success or failure), and/or add a deadline after which the next setSetting flushes and clears the flag.

### [MEDIUM] 34 registerSettingsModule() call sites against MAX_SETTINGS_MODULES 32 - a full-feature build silently drops the last modules, whose settings then never load and never save
- **Where:** `components/hardwareone/System_Settings.cpp:2029`
- **What:** MAX_SETTINGS_MODULES is 32 (System_Settings.h:1321) and gSettingsModules is sized to it (:2023). registerAllSettingsModules() contains 34 registerSettingsModule() calls (:2126-2224, verified by count). The overflow branch (:2029-2032) logs ERROR_SYSTEMF("Max settings modules reached") and returns - no assert, no boot failure, no user-visible signal. The last two registered are llmSettingsModule (:2218) and mapsSettingsModule (:2223).
- **Why it matters:** A dropped module is invisible to readRegisteredSettings/writeRegisteredSettings/applyRegisteredDefaults (all iterate gSettingsModules), so every one of its settings runs on the C++ struct initializer forever: writes via its commands mutate RAM and are never serialized, and any values already on disk become an orphaned section that writeSettingsJson's merge-read faithfully preserves but nothing ever applies. The result is a whole feature's configuration that appears to save and silently reverts on every boot. Not all 34 are simultaneously live on the primary FeatherS3 config (I2C_FEATURE_LEVEL 4 with most sensors off, MQTT/SR/EI/LLM off, camera off) - I counted ~20 there, so this is latent on the primary board. It is reachable on a full build: I2C_FEATURE_LEVEL 3 turns on thermal/tof/imu/apds/gps/fmradio/rtc/presence, and ENABLE_MQTT / ENABLE_ESP_SR / ENABLE_EDGE_IMPULSE / ENABLE_ONDEVICE_LLM / camera + mic are each independently switchable in System_BuildConfig.h. Note also that inputSettingsModule and anoEncoderSettingsModule are NOT mutually exclusive at registration: ENABLE_OLED_INPUT is (GAMEPAD || ANO) (System_BuildConfig.h:484), so selecting INPUT_DEVICE_TYPE 2 registers both.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED — board: board-gated - not reachable on the current FeatherS3 config (~20 modules register); reachable on a full-sensor + MQTT + SR/EI/LLM build
- **Direction:** Derive the cap from the call sites rather than hand-picking it - e.g. raise MAX_SETTINGS_MODULES with headroom and add a static_assert or a boot-time check that gSettingsModuleCount equals the number of attempted registrations, so a future module cannot be dropped silently.

### [MEDIUM] Stack-overflow detection is canary-only with the end-of-stack watchpoint disabled, while the image contains 105 functions with frames >= 700 B that can jump the 16-byte canary
- **Where:** `sdkconfig:1977`
- **Also:** sdkconfig:2008 (# CONFIG_FREERTOS_WATCHPOINT_END_OF_STACK is not set), sdkconfig:733 CONFIG_COMPILER_STACK_CHECK_MODE_NORM, sdkconfig:62 CONFIG_ESP_TASK_WDT_PANIC not set
- **What:** sdkconfig has CONFIG_FREERTOS_CHECK_STACKOVERFLOW_CANARY=y and, at :2008, CONFIG_FREERTOS_WATCHPOINT_END_OF_STACK is not set. The canary method fills the low end of each stack with a pattern and compares only the first 16 bytes, and only at context-switch time. Frame-size census of build/hardwareone-idf.elf (12,248 functions with an Xtensa `entry` prologue): 2 functions >= 3904 B, 8 >= 2000 B, 52 >= 1000 B, 105 >= 700 B, 255 >= 512 B. Largest reachable-from-firmware examples: lc3_mdct_inverse 3904, sanitizeAutomationsJson 2224, renderGameOfLifeAnimation 2096, systemEventsNotifyTick / systemEventLogTick 1792 each, runQ30ListTextImageProbe 1776, handleAudioNotify 1728, schedulerTickMinute 1680, handleGPSTracksAPI 1648, handleEvents 1600.
- **Why it matters:** This is pitfall P10 in its exact textbook form. A function whose prologue drops the stack pointer by 700–3900 bytes in one `entry` instruction never touches the canary region on the way past it; it starts writing locals at an address already below the stack allocation, i.e. inside whatever heap block or adjacent task stack follows. FreeRTOS then finds an intact canary at the next context switch and reports nothing. The result is a memory corruption that surfaces later, somewhere unrelated — the classic "crashes only sometimes, only under load" signature this project has repeatedly chased. CONFIG_COMPILER_STACK_CHECK_MODE_NORM=y is on and does help (verified: __stack_chk_guard loads are present in captureEspNowFrame and fsmWorkerTask prologues), but -fstack-protector only guards arrays of >8 bytes at function *return*, so it catches an overrun of a local buffer while missing an overrun of the frame as a whole, and it reports only after the writes have already landed.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED — independently reported by 2 finders
- **Direction:** Consider enabling CONFIG_FREERTOS_WATCHPOINT_END_OF_STACK during the pre-1.0 hardening window: it costs one of the two hardware watchpoints and makes an overflow fault at the instant of the first out-of-bounds write, naming the offending task. Weigh it against any existing use of the debug watchpoints; it is a debug-build lever, not necessarily a ship setting.

### [MEDIUM] Brownout trip point is 2.44 V — 0.56 V below the ESP32-S3's minimum recommended supply — and hardware auto-reset is disabled, so undervoltage surfaces as panics/WDTs rather than ESP_RST_BROWNOUT
- **Where:** `sdkconfig:1571`
- **What:** `CONFIG_ESP_BROWNOUT_DET_LVL_SEL_7=y` / `CONFIG_ESP_BROWNOUT_DET_LVL=7`. Per esp-idf/components/esp_hw_support/power_supply/port/esp32s3/Kconfig.power, level 7 is the *lowest* threshold in the table (2.44 V; the table runs 2.44/2.56/2.67/2.84/2.98/3.19/3.30 V) and is the IDF default. No project file pins a brownout level — it appears in neither sdkconfig.defaults, nor sdkconfig.defaults.esp32s3/.esp32, nor boards/*.defaults — so this was never a decision. Separately, `CONFIG_SPI_FLASH_BROWNOUT_RESET_XMC=y` (sdkconfig:2543) selects SPI_FLASH_BROWNOUT_RESET which selects `ESP_BROWNOUT_USE_INTR` (esp-idf/components/spi_flash/Kconfig:19); that path configures the detector with `.reset_enabled = false` (esp-idf/components/esp_hw_support/power_supply/brownout.c:88-92) and relies entirely on an ISR to call esp_rom_software_reset_system().
- **Why it matters:** The ESP32-S3's recommended operating supply floor is 3.0 V. On a LiPo-powered FeatherS3 a sagging rail spends the whole 3.0 V -> 2.44 V band running the CPU, radio and flash out of spec with no detector trip: what the user sees is random panics, interrupt/task WDT resets, or corrupted LittleFS writes. The firmware's own attribution machinery then labels those `panic` / `int_wdt` (HardwareOne.cpp:1256-1276, :1419-1428) and increments crashCount — i.e. reports a power problem as a firmware bug, which is exactly the P45 failure mode. The interrupt-based path compounds it: interrupts are disabled for the duration of a flash erase/write (spi_flash_disable_interrupts_caches_and_other_cpu), so a brownout during the single most corruption-sensitive window cannot be serviced, and with reset_enabled=false the hardware will not step in either.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED — board: all; the voltage table differs on ESP32-classic (esp32/Kconfig.power) so the classic boards need their own level chosen; prior art: partially — docs/ESP32_PITFALL_AUDIT.md P45 lists the check and recon_prior.md states "brownout config itself unaudited"; project_powersave_crash_loop covers a different cause (setCpuFrequencyMhz vs g
- **Direction:** Pin an explicit brownout level in sdkconfig.defaults at or above the chip's operating floor (SEL_3 = 2.98 V or SEL_4 = 2.84 V) so undervoltage produces a clean, attributable ESP_RST_BROWNOUT instead of a mystery panic. Also consider logging the battery voltage alongside the existing BOOT/`SYSEVT_CRASH` record so a crash can be correlated with supply state after the fact.

### [MEDIUM] CONFIG_SPI_FLASH_ERASE_YIELD_DURATION_MS does not bound a single sector erase — every 4 KB erase stalls BOTH cores for the full erase time
- **Where:** `sdkconfig:2560`
- **Also:** sdkconfig:2579-2582 (yield knobs), sdkconfig:1790-1798 (INT_WDT 1500 ms, TWDT 5 s / panic off)
- **What:** `CONFIG_SPI_FLASH_AUTO_SUSPEND` is not set (sdkconfig:2560), so every flash program/erase runs `spi_flash_disable_interrupts_caches_and_other_cpu()`. I traced that function in the pinned IDF (`~/esp/esp-idf/components/spi_flash/cache_utils.c:125-193`): it calls `vTaskSuspendAll()` on the calling core, IPCs `spi_flash_op_block_func` onto the other core (which also suspends its scheduler and spins), calls `esp_intr_noniram_disable()`, then disables both caches. The mitigation the config *appears* to provide — `CONFIG_SPI_FLASH_YIELD_DURING_ERASE=y`, `ERASE_YIELD_DURATION_MS=20`, `ERASE_YIELD_TICKS=1` (sdkconfig:2579-2581) — is only applied BETWEEN erase commands: the `chip->chip_drv->yield()` call sits at the top of `esp_flash_erase_region`'s `while(1)` loop (`esp_flash_api.c:663-667`), outside `start()`/`end()`. `spi_flash_chip_generic_erase_sector` (`spi_flash_chip_generic.c:212-233`) issues the erase and then busy-polls `spi_flash_chip_generic_wait_idle` (:424-480), which contains no yield at all — only `esp_rom_delay_us` spins.
- **Why it matters:** A single LittleFS block erase — triggered by roughly every 4 KB of new file data, every metadata-block compaction, and every wear-levelling relocation — freezes BOTH cores, suspends all task scheduling, and masks every non-IRAM interrupt for the whole erase. On the 16 MB parts this build auto-detects (`ESPTOOLPY_FLASH_MODE_AUTO_DETECT=y`, seven vendor drivers enabled at sdkconfig:2597-2603) that is ~30-50 ms typical and up to the datasheet max (400 ms on common W25Q/GD25Q parts). Everything in findings 2-6 is a consequence of this window, not of a 20 ms one. Nothing in the tree documents the real bound.
- **Confidence:** CONFIRMED
- **Direction:** Treat ~50 ms (worst case a few hundred) as the real per-erase stall budget when reasoning about any real-time path, and record that number somewhere durable. Do NOT reach for CONFIG_SPI_FLASH_AUTO_SUSPEND as the fix — it is chip-dependent and experimental; reducing erase *frequency* on the hot paths (findings 2-5) is the tractable lever.

### [MEDIUM] CONFIG_ESP_INT_WDT_TIMEOUT_MS=1500 (5x the IDF default) — its only written justification names a driver that IDF v5.5 compiles out
- **Where:** `sdkconfig.defaults:122`
- **Also:** sdkconfig:1791 (live value); components/hardwareone/System_WiFi.cpp:1088-1094 (same stale I2C_ENTER_CRITICAL/periph_spinlock rationale); components/hardwareone/System_I2C_Manager.cpp:288 (comment that reasons off the 1500 ms number)
- **What:** The comment reads: "Interrupt watchdog - 1500ms gives headroom for I2C FSM reset under HTTPS load. The I2C driver's i2c_hw_fsm_reset enters nested spinlocks (I2C + periph clock)". i2c_hw_fsm_reset is a static function in ESP-IDF's LEGACY driver (esp-idf/components/driver/i2c/i2c.c:714), reached only through arduino-esp32's esp32-hal-i2c.c.
